THE SECOND RATIONAL HOMOLOGY OF THE TORELLI GROUP IS FINITELY GENERATED
Daniel Minahan

Abstract
. We prove that second rational homology of the Torelli group of an orientable closed surface of genus g g is finite dimensional for g ≥ 51 g\geq 51 . This rules out the simplest obstruction to the Torelli group being finitely presented and provides a partial answer to a question of Bestvina.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 54C40, 14E20; Secondary 49E25, 20C20

Historical Remark
. This is a permanent preprint. The results of this paper have been subsumed and significantly improved upon by the work of the author and Putman
[
MinahanPutmanH2
]
.

1.
Introduction

Let
S_{g}^{b}
be a compact, orientable surface of genus
g
with
b
boundary components. The
mapping class group

\Mod(S_{g})
is
\pi_{0}(\Diff^{+}(S_{g}))
. The action of
\Mod(S_{g})
on
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
induces a representation
\Mod(S_{g})\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
called the
symplectic representation
, where
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
is the group of invertible linear transformations of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
that respect the algebraic intersection form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. The kernel of this representation is called the
Torelli group
and is denoted
\mathcal{I}_{g}
. There is a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\Mod(S_{g})\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1.

By the work of McCullough–Miller
[
19
]
, Mess
[
20
]
and Johnson
[
12
]
, the Torelli group is finitely generated if and only if
g\neq 2
. Birman posed the following question
[
4
, Problem 29]
:

Question 1
.

Is
\mathcal{I}_{g}
finitely presented for any sufficiently large
g
?

Similar questions were asked by Mess
[
16
, Page 90]
and Morita
[
22
, Problem 2.1]
.

Obstructions to finite presentability
. If
G
is a finitely generated group with
H_{2}(G;\mathbb{Q})
infinite dimensional, then
G
is not finitely presentable. The main result of the paper is the following theorem, which shows that
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
does not obstruct finite presentability of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
for
g\gg 0
.

Theorem A
.

Let
g\geq 51
. The vector space
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional.

Theorem
A
partially answers the following question of Bestvina
[
7
, Page 5]
. Margalit also asked a similar question
[
18
, Question 5.12]
).

Question 2
.

For which choices of
k,g
, and commutative ring
R
is
H_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{g};R)
finitely generated?

Prior partial answers to Question
2
.
Mess showed that
\mathcal{I}_{2}
is an infinitely generated free group
[
20
]
, which implies that
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{2})
is infinitely generated and
H_{i}(\mathcal{I}_{2})=0
for
i\geq 2
. Johnson
[
13
]
computed
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})
for
g\geq 3
and proved that

H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}).

In fact, Johnson defined a map
\tau_{g}:\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
now called the Johnson homomorphism, and showed that
(\tau_{g})_{*}
induces an isomorphism in first rational homology
[
13
]
. Akita showed that
H_{*}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})
is infinitely generated as an abelian group for
g\geq 7

[
1
]
. Hain showed that
H_{3}(\mathcal{I}_{3};\mathbb{Z})
is infinitely generated
[
10
]
. Bestvina, Bux, and Margalit showed that the cohomological dimension of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
is
3g-5

[
2
, Theorem A]
, which implies that
H_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})=0
for
k\geq 3g-4
. In this same paper, they proved that
H_{3g-5}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})
is infinitely generated for
g\geq 2

[
2
, Theorem C]
. Gaifullin later showed that
H_{k}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})
is infinitely generated for
2g-3\leq k\leq 3g-5

[
9
]
, confirming a conjecture of Bestvina–Bux–Margalit
[
2
]
.

1.1.
The outline of the proof of Theorem
A

Theorem
A
will follow from the following result.

Theorem B
.

Let
g\geq 51
. Let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. The cokernel of the pushforward map
\iota_{*}:H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional.

Most of the paper will be devoted to proving Theorem
B
.

The connection between Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
. What follows is a trick. We will expand this trick in Proposition
2.1
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a finite collection of nonseparating simple closed curves such that the set of Dehn twists

\{T_{c}:c\in\mathcal{B}\}

generates
\Mod(S_{g})
, e.g., the curves corresponding to the Humphries generators
[
8
]
. Consider the map

\varphi:H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{c\in\mathcal{B}}\cok(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

Now, for any
c\in\mathcal{B}
, the quotient map
\rho_{c}:H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\cok(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
satisfies

\ker(\rho_{c})=\im(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

Hence we have

\ker(\varphi)=\bigcap_{c\in\mathcal{B}}\im(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

Then for any
c\in\mathcal{B}
,
T_{c}
acts trivially
\im(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Therefore every element of
\{T_{c}:c\in\mathcal{B}\}
acts trivially on
\ker(\varphi)
. Since
\{T_{c}:c\in\mathcal{B}\}
generates
\Mod(S_{g})
, the vector space
\ker(\varphi)
is a trivial
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
–module. We will show in Lemma
9.1
that
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional. This is a result of Kassabov and Putman
[
14
]
, for which we provide an alternative proof. Theorem
B
says that each vector space
\cok(H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(c);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional. Since
\mathcal{B}
is a finite set, the vector space
\im(\varphi)
is finite dimensional. Consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\ker(\varphi)\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\im(\varphi)\rightarrow 0.

A portion of the corresponding long exact sequence in
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
–homology is given by

\ldots\rightarrow H_{1}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});\im(\varphi))\rightarrow H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});\ker(\varphi))\rightarrow H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\rightarrow\ldots

Since
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
is finitely generated and
\im(\varphi)
is finite dimensional,
H_{1}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});\im(\varphi))
is finite dimensional as well. By Lemma
9.1
,
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional. Hence the vector space
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}),\ker(\varphi))
is finite dimensional. Then
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});\ker(\varphi))
is isomorphic to
\ker(\varphi)
because
\ker(\varphi)
is a trivial
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
–module, so
\ker(\varphi)
is finite dimensional. Since
\im(\varphi)
is finite dimensional as well, we have
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
finite dimensional.

1.2.
The proof of Theorem
B

The proof of Theorem
B
proceeds by considering the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on a complex called the complex of homologous curves, and then studying the equivariant homology spectral sequence for this group action.

The complex of homologous curves
. In this paper, a
curve
on a surface
S
is an isotopy class of oriented essential embedded copies of
S^{1}
. Two curves
c
and
d
are
disjoint
if they have disjoint representatives. The
geometric intersection number
, denoted
|c\cap d|
, is the minimum number of intersection points over all representatives of
c
and
d
. Let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating curve and let
[a]\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
denote the corresponding first homology class. The
complex of homologous curves

\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is the complex where the vertices are curves
c
such that
c
represents
[a]
in
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
. A
k
–cell in
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is a collection of
k+1
pairwise disjoint vertices of
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. A theorem of the author says that
\widetilde{H}_{k}(C_{[a]}(S_{g});\mathbb{Z})=0
for
0\leq k\leq g-3

[
21
, Theorem A]
.

The equivariant homology spectral sequences
. We consider the equivariant homology spectral sequence
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})

[
5
, Section VII]
. Let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating curve and let
X_{g}=\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g}
. We will use the notation
X_{g}
throughout the paper to refer to this quotient. If
\sigma
is a cell of
X_{g}
, let
(\mathcal{I}_{g})_{\sigma}
denote the stabilizer
\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{\sigma})
for some arbitrary lift of
\sigma
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. It is known that if
x
and
y
are nonseparating homologous curves in
S_{g}
, then there is an
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
such that
fx=y
(see, e.g.,
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
). Hence the set
X_{g}^{(0)}
of vertices is a singleton. It follows that page 1 of
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
is as in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Page 1 of
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. All coefficients are in
\mathbb{Q}
.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is 2–acyclic for
g\geq 5

[
21
, Theorem A]
, the spectral sequence
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
converges to
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g})

[
5
, Section VII]
. Examining Figure
1
, we see that the cokernel of the map

\iota_{*}:H_{2}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is noncanonically identified with the direct sum

\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{\infty}\oplus\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{\infty}.

Hence the cokernel of
\iota_{*}
is noncanonically identified with a subquotient of

\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}.

The proof of Theorem
B
will proceed in two steps:

I.

the vector space
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}
is finite dimensional, and

II.

the vector space
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is finite dimensional.

By definition,
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}
is canonically identified with
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Step I is recorded as the following proposition.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
X_{g}=\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g}
. The vector space
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional.

Proposition
1.1
will be proven in Sections
3
–
6
. Step II of the proof of Theorem
B
is recorded as the following result.

Proposition 1.2
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence in rational coefficients for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. The vector space
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is finite dimensional.

Proposition
1.2
will be proven in Sections
7
–
8
. Before discussing the proofs of Propositions
1.1
and
1.2
, we will discuss the main technical tool of the paper.

1.3.
The main technical tool of the paper

We will now explain the main technical tool that we use to prove Theorem
A
, Proposition
1.1
, and Proposition
1.2
. A specific instance of this tool has already been discussed in Section
1.1
.

Notation
. Throughout this paper,
\Stab
will be taken to mean the pointwise stabilizer, as opposed to the setwise stabilizer.

1
Proposition
2.1
.

Let
g\geq 1
, and let
N\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve such that
|N|<g
. Let
G\subseteq\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
be the image of the map
\Stab_{\Mod(S_{g})}(N)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
. Let
V
be a
G
–representation over
\mathbb{Q}
. Suppose that there is a constant
0\leq d\leq g-|N|
such that the following hold:

(1)

For any
M\subseteq S_{g}
such that:

•

|M|\geq d
,

•

M
is disjoint from
N
, and

•

M\sqcup N
is nonseparating,

the cokernel of the map
\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is finite dimensional.

(2)

For any
M\subseteq S_{g}
such that:

•

|M|<d
,

•

M
is disjoint from
N
, and

•

M\sqcup N
is nonseparating,

the coinvariants module
V_{\Stab_{G}(M)}
is finite dimensional.

Then
V
is finite dimensional.

In practice, we will often show that the first hypothesis is satisfied by showing that the natural map
\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is surjective. We will prove Proposition
2.1
in Section
2
.

1.4.
The outline of the paper

The paper is organized into four chunks.

•

Section
2
, which contains the proof of Proposition
2.1
.

•

Sections
3
–
6
, which contain the proof of Proposition
1.1
.

•

Sections
7
and
8
, which contain the proof of Proposition
1.2
. Additionally, Section
8
contains the proof of Theorem
B
.

•

Section
9
, which contains the proof of Theorem
A
.

We now explain the organization of the second and third chunk in more detail.

Sections
3
–
6
, which prove Proposition
1.1
. Our goal is to prove Proposition
1.1
by applying Proposition
2.1
with
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x})
,
d=1
, and
V=H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
(recall that
X_{g}=\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g}
). In particular, if
a\subseteq S_{g}
is a representative of
\vec{x}
, we will prove the following:

(1)

For any primitive
c
disjoint from and not homologous to
a
, the cokernel of
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{[c]}}\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional, and

(2)

the coinvariants module
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G}
is finite dimensional.

The latter property is not too difficult to prove, while the former property is the bulk of the work of Sections
3
–
6
. The proof of the first hypothesis proceeds in the following steps:

(1)

Construct a subspace spanned by fundamental classes of tori
T\subseteq X_{g}
called Bestvina–Margalit tori. These tori are constructed in Section
3
, and the subspace spanned by their fundamental classes is denoted
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
.

(2)

Show that for any primitive
c\subseteq S_{g}
disjoint from and inhomologous to
a
, the cokernel
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{[c]}}\rightarrow\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is spanned by classes in
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. This is the bulk of the work of Section
3
, and is stated as Lemma
6.1
.

(3)

Use Proposition
2.1
with
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x},[c])
,
d=9
, and
V
the cokernel of the map
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{[c]}}\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. This is carried out in two substeps, which each verify a hypothesis of Proposition
2.1
.

(a)

The subspace
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the image of a subspace
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\subseteq H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
generated by abelian cycles consisting of bounding pair maps. The main work of Section
4
is Proposition
4.1
, which says that for a nonseparating multicurve
M\subseteq S_{g}
containing at least 9 curves, the map

\bigoplus_{d\subseteq M}\left(H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)^{T_{[d]}}\rightarrow H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is surjective. Since
V=H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{c}}
is a quotient of
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
by Lemma
6.1
and
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is a quotient of
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, the map

\rho:\bigoplus_{d\subseteq M}V^{T_{[d]}}\rightarrow V

is surjective. In particular, this implies that
\cok(\rho)
is finite dimensional, so hypothesis (1) of Proposition
2.1
is satisfied for
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x},c)
,
V=H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{c}}
and
d=9
.

(b)

We now show that for
M
a multicurve with
|M|\leq 8
such that
M
is disjoint from
a
and
c
and
a\sqcup c\sqcup M
is nonseparating, the coinvariants module

V_{\Stab_{[M]}G}

is finite dimensional, where
[M]
denotes the set of homology classes represented by elements of
M
. Since
V
is a quotient of
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
by Lemma
6.1
, it suffices to show that
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{\Stab_{[M]}G}
is finite dimensional. This is the content of Lemma
5.1
and is the main work of Section
5
.

Statements (a) and (b) are the hypotheses of Proposition
2.1
for
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x},[c])
,
d=9
, and
V
the cokernel of the map
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{[c]}}\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Hence
V
is finite dimensional by Proposition
2.1
.

Hence for any primitive
c
disjoint from and not homologous to
a
, the cokernel of
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{[c]}}\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional. If
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x})
then it is not too difficult to show that
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G}
is finite dimensional. Hence by applying Proposition
2.1
with
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x})
,
d=1
, and
V=H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, we conclude that
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional, which is the statement of Proposition
1.1
.

Sections
7
and
8
, which prove Proposition
1.2
. The approach is to apply Proposition
2.1
with
G=\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}(\vec{x})
,
d=8
, and
V=\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
. We verify each hypothesis of Proposition
2.1
in turn.

(1)

Hypothesis (1) is stated as Lemma
8.1
, and is the main content of Section
8.1
.

(2)

Hypothesis (2) is stated as Lemma
7.1
and is the main content of Section
8
.

Given these two results, we prove Proposition
1.2
by applying Proposition
2.1
. Additionally, Section
8
contains the proof of Theorem
B
.

2.
A finiteness result about groups acting on vector spaces

The goal in this section is to prove Proposition
2.1
, which is a result that we will apply repeatedly to determine that certain representations of subgroups of the symplectic group are finite dimensional. The statement is as follows.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
g\geq 1
, and let
N\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve such that
|N|<g
. Let
G\subseteq\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
be the image of the map
\Stab_{\Mod(S_{g})}(N)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
. Let
V
be a
G
–representation over
\mathbb{Q}
. Suppose that there is a constant
0\leq d\leq g-|N|
such that the following hold:

(1)

For any
M\subseteq S_{g}
such that:

•

|M|\geq d
,

•

M
is disjoint from
N
, and

•

M\sqcup N
is nonseparating,

the cokernel of the map
\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is finite dimensional.

(2)

For any
M\subseteq S_{g}
such that:

•

|M|<d
,

•

M
is disjoint from
N
, and

•

M\sqcup N
is nonseparating,

the coinvariants module
V_{\Stab_{G}(M)}
is finite dimensional.

Then
V
is finite dimensional.

Before proving the proposition, we will need a result about generating sets of subgroups of the symplectic group, which is Lemma
2.2
. We will also standardize some terminology that we will use throughout the paper.

Terminology on symplectic lattices
. A
quasi–unimodular lattice
is a finitely generated free abelian group
L
equipped with an alternating form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:L\times L\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The lattice
L
is
unimodular
if the form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is nondegenerate. The
genus of

L
, denoted
g(L)
, is
\frac{1}{2}\rk(W)
, where
W\subseteq L
is a maximal free abelian subgroup of
L
such that the restriction of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to
W
is nondegenerate. If
v\in L
is some element, then
v
is
primitive
if there is no integer
m\geq 2
and nonzero
w\in L
with
mw=v
. For any
v\in L
, the
transvection along

v
is the homomorphism
T_{v}:L\rightarrow L
given by

T_{v}(w)=w+\langle v,w\rangle v.

We will say that the transvection
T_{v}
is
primitive
if
v
is primitive. We will say that a subgroup
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
is
primitive
if
L
is generated by primitive elements. The set of degenerate elements in
L
, i.e., elements
v\in L
such that
\langle v,\cdot\rangle:L\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the zero map, will be denoted
L^{\degen}
. If
\mathcal{V}\subseteq L
is a set of elements, we will denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\perp}
the subgroup
\{w\in L:\langle w,v\rangle=0\text{ for all }v\in\mathcal{V}\}
. We let

\Sp(L,\mathbb{Z})=\{g\in\GL(L,\mathbb{Z}):\langle gv,gw\rangle=\langle v,w\rangle\text{ for all }v,w\in L\}.

If
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
is a primitive, unimodular subgroup, let
\proj_{L^{\prime}}:L\rightarrow L^{\prime}
denote the projection map induced by the form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.

Multicurves
. We will say that a
multicurve

M\subseteq S_{g}
is an isotopy class of pairwise disjoint oriented curves. We will use the notation
\pi_{0}(M)
to denote the set of isotopy classes of curves in
M
. The stabilizer group
\Stab_{\Mod(S_{g})}(M)
will denote the subgroup of
\Mod(S_{g})
consisting of elements that stabilize each curve
c\in\pi_{0}(M)
. Note that since our multicurves are oriented, the stabilizer
\Stab_{\Mod(S_{g})}(M)
does not change the orientation of the curves of
M
.

Notation
. For the remainder of the paper, we will use
{\ssearrow}
to denote Farb and Margalit’s notion of cutting along curves on surfaces
[
8
, Section 3.6]
.

Mapping class groups of cut open surfaces
. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve. The mapping class group
\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M)
is the mapping class group of the cut open surface. There is a natural inclusion
S_{g}{\ssearrow}M\hookrightarrow M
, and this induces a map
\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M)\rightarrow\Mod(S_{g})
given by extending
\varphi\in\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M)
by the identity along
S_{g}\setminus\im(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M\rightarrow S_{g})
.

We are now ready to state and prove a preparatory lemma, after which we will conclude Section
2
by proving Proposition
2.1
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
g\geq 2
and
b\geq 0
, and let
S=S_{g}^{b}
be an oriented, compact surface of genus
g
with
b
boundary components. Let
M\subseteq S
be a nonseparating multicurve such that
g(S{\ssearrow}M)\geq 1
. Let
G
be the image of the composition

\Mod(S{\ssearrow}M)\rightarrow\Mod(S)\rightarrow\Aut(H_{1}(S_{g}^{b};\mathbb{Z})).

Then
G
has a finite generating set consisting of transvections along classes
v
represented by nonseparating curves
c\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}M
.

Proof.

Korkmaz
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
has shown that if
S_{g^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}}
is a compact, oriented surface with genus
g^{\prime}\geq 1
, then
\Mod(S_{g^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}})
has a finite generating set
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Mod(S_{g^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}})
consisting of Dehn twists along nonseparating curves. Then
S{\ssearrow}M
is a compact, oriented surface, and has genus at least one by hypothesis. Hence there is a finite set of Dehn twists along nonseparating curves
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\Mod(S{\ssearrow}M)
such that
\mathcal{D}
generates
\Mod(S{\ssearrow}M)
. Then the image of
T_{d}\in\mathcal{D}
under the symplectic representation is a transvection along
[d]
. Hence
G
has a finite generating set transvections along elements represented by nonseparating curves, as desired.
∎

We are now ready to conclude Section
2
.

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

The proof proceeds by induction on
d
.

Base case:
d=0
. In this case, the first hypothesis says that if
M
is the empty multicurve, then the cokernel
\bigoplus_{c\in\emptyset}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is finite dimensional. This is just saying that the cokernel of the zero map is finite dimensional, so in particular
V
is finite dimensional.

Inductive step:
d\geq 1
. Assume that the proposition holds for all
d^{\prime}<d
. We will show that it holds for
d
as well. Our aim is to show that if
G
and
V
satisfy the hypotheses of the proposition for
d
, then they satisfy the hypotheses of the proposition for
d-1
as well. The inductive hypothesis would then imply that
V
is finite dimensional. The second hypothesis, namely that the coinvariants module
V_{\Stab_{G}(M)}
is finite dimensional for
|M|<d
also holds for
|M|<d-1
, so it suffices to prove the first hypothesis for
d-1
. It suffices to show that for
M
a multicurve with:

•

|M|\geq d-1
,

•

M
is disjoint from
N
, and

•

M\sqcup N
is nonseparating,

the cokernel of the map
\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is finite dimensional. This holds for
|M|\geq d
by hypothesis, so it suffices to show that the result holds for
|M|=d-1
.

Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}N
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|M\right|=d-1
. Let
G^{\prime}=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}(M\sqcup N)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. By Lemma
2.2
,
G"
is generated by a finite set of transvections
\mathcal{F}
along a set of primitive elements
\mathcal{V}
such that each
v\in\mathcal{V}
has a nonseparating representative
c\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}M
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of such representatives, one for each element of
\mathcal{V}
. Now, for any
c\in\mathcal{R}
, let
M_{c}=M\sqcup c
. The multicurve
M_{c}
satisfies:

•

|M_{c}|=1+|M|=d
,

•

M_{c}
is disjoint from
N
since
M
and
c
are, and

•

M_{c}\sqcup N
is nonseparating since
c
is nonseparating on
S_{g}{\ssearrow}(M\sqcup N)
by our choice of generating set.

Therefore, the first hypothesis in the proposition applied to
V
as a
G
representation tells us that

\cok(\bigoplus_{d\in M_{c}}V^{T_{[d]}}\rightarrow V)

is finite dimensional. In particular, this implies that, if we let
V_{M}=\cok(\bigoplus_{d\in M}V^{T_{d}}\rightarrow V)
, then
V_{M}^{T_{v}}
has finite codimension in
V_{M}
for any
v\in\mathcal{V}
. Now, consider the filtration of
V_{M}
given by ordering
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and setting
W_{M,i}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}V_{M}^{T_{v_{i}}}
. Since
V_{M}^{T_{v_{i}}}
has finite codimension in
V_{M}
, each
V_{M,i}
has finite codimension in
V_{M,i-1}
. In particular, this implies that
W_{M,n}
has finite codimension in
V_{M}
. Thus, it suffices to show that
W_{M,n}
is finite dimensional.

W_{M,n}
is finite dimensional
. Consider the long exact sequence in
G^{\prime}
–homology associated to the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow W_{M,n}\rightarrow V_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}/W_{M,n}\rightarrow 0.

Part of this sequence is given by

H_{1}(G^{\prime};V_{M}/W_{M,n})\rightarrow H_{0}(G^{\prime};W_{M,n})\rightarrow H_{0}(G^{\prime};V_{M}).

The group
G^{\prime}
is finitely presented by Lemma
2.2
and
V_{M}/W_{M,n}
is finite dimensional by hypothesis, so
H_{1}(G^{\prime};V_{M}/W_{M,n})
is finite dimensional. Furthermore,
H_{0}(G^{\prime};V_{M})
is a quotient of
H_{0}(G^{\prime};V)
. This last vector space is finite dimensional by applying the second hypothesis of the proposition to the multicurve
M
, since we know
|M|=d-1<d
. Therefore, the vector space
H_{0}(G^{\prime};W_{M,n})
is finite dimensional. But now,
W_{M,n}
is the intersection
\bigcap_{v\in\mathcal{V}}V_{M}^{T_{v}}
. Since the set
\mathcal{F}=\{T_{v}:v\in\mathcal{V}\}
generates
G^{\prime}
by assumption, we conclude that
W_{M,n}
is a trivial
G^{\prime}
–representation, so
H_{0}(G^{\prime};W_{M,n})=W_{M,n}
. Hence
W_{M,n}
is finite dimensional and has finite codimension in
V_{M}
, so
V_{M}
is finite dimensional as well. The proof is now complete by the inductive hypothesis that the proposition holds for
d-1
.
∎

3.
Bestvina–Margalit tori

For the remainder of this section, fix a
g\geq 3
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating curve. Let
X_{g}=\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g}
. A Bestvina–Margalit torus (defined below), is a certain type of 2–torus embedded in
X_{g}
. There is a subspace
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\subseteq H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
generated by the fundamental classes of the Bestvina–Margalit tori. The goal in this section is to prove basic structural results about
X_{g}
and Bestvina–Margalit tori.

Bestvina–Margalit tori, by example
. In unpublished work
[
3
]
, Bestvina and Margalit associate to each 2-cell
\sigma
in
X_{g}
a corresponding dual cell. The cells
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
form a torus which we refer to as a
Bestvina–Margalit torus
. The idea is that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
have the same edges, except the edges are in a different cyclic order. Let
x,y,z,w\in\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
be as in Figure
2
. Let
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
be the 2-cell such that there is a lift
\widehat{\sigma}\subseteq C_{[a]}(S_{g})
with vertices
x,y,
and
w
, and let
\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq X_{g}
be the 2–cell with a lift
\widehat{\sigma}^{\prime}
with vertices
x,z,
and
w
. Then the edges
e_{x,y}
and
e_{y,w}
are in the same orbit under the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
to
e_{z,w}
and
e_{x,z}
, respectively
[
24
, Proposition 3.29]
. However, the cells
\widehat{\sigma}
and
\widehat{\sigma}^{\prime}
are not in the same orbit under the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
, since they induce decompositions of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
with different cyclic orders. Hence the cells
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
form a torus in
X_{g}
.

x
y
w
z

Figure 2.
The construction of dual cells.

We will denote this torus
\BM_{\sigma}
and its fundamental class by
[\BM_{\sigma}]
. In general, a
Bestvina–Margalit
torus is any subcomplex of
X_{g}
given by the union of a pair of 2–cells
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
such that each edge of
\sigma
is in the same
\mathcal{I}_{g}
–orbit as an edge of
\sigma^{\prime}
, but
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
. Let
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
denote the subspace of
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
generated by the set of fundamental classes
\{[\BM_{\sigma}]\}_{\sigma\in X_{g}^{(2)}}
. This subspace plays a crucial role in the proof of Proposition
1.1
.

3.1.
Structural results about
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g}

In this section we will prove Lemma
3.1
, which will tell us when two
k
–cells
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
are in the same
\mathcal{I}_{g}
–orbit for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g}).
It is known that two nonseparating curves
c
and
d
in
S_{g}
are in the same orbit under the action of the Torelli group if and only if
c
and
d
are homologous (see, e.g., Putman
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
). Hence the complex
X_{g}
has one vertex. Since
X_{g}
has one vertex, there is a group generated by the edges of
X_{g}
with relations given by 2–cells. We will use this fact to denote the composition of two edges
y
and
z
that share a 2–cell by
yz
, where this edge is the third edge of the 2–cell. This specifies an orientation on the edges, since there is one orientation of the edges
y
and
z
such that
y*z=yz
, where
*
is the composition in the group generated by the edges on
X_{g}
. If
e\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is an edge, there is a bounding pair
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
taking one endpoint of
e
to the other endpoint. See Figure
3
for an example.

v
w
c
c^{\prime}

Figure 3.
The bounding pair map
T_{c}T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}
and edge
e=e_{v,w}
.

Lemma
3.1
describes the cells of the complex
X_{g}
. Before stating and proving the lemma, we introduce some notation. Let
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
be a
k
–cell with a lift
\widehat{\sigma}\subset\mathcal{C}{[a]}(S_{g})
, where
\widehat{\sigma}
corresponds to a multicurve
a_{0}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup a_{k}
. Let
S_{0},\ldots,S_{k}
be the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{\sigma}
. Assume that
S_{i}\cap S_{j}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
i-j\equiv\pm 1\mo k+1
. For each
S_{i}
, let
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma}
denote the image in
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
of the pushforward map
H_{1}(S_{i};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
denote the set of free abelian groups
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}
. Associated to
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is a directed cycle
C
. The vertices of
C
are the connected components
S_{i}
and there is a directed edge
S_{i}\rightarrow S_{j}
if the following hold:

•

a_{\ell}\subseteq S_{i}\cap S_{j}
for some
0\leq\ell\leq k
, and

•

a_{\ell}
is oriented so that
S_{i}
is on the left of
a_{\ell}
.

This directed cycle induces a cyclic ordering on the set
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
. For convenience, we will index the
\mathcal{H}_{i}
so that the cyclic order is
\mathcal{H}_{0}<\mathcal{H}_{1}<\ldots<\mathcal{H}_{k}<\mathcal{H}_{0}
. We will refer to this cyclically ordered set
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
as the
\textit{cyclic decomposition of }H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z}){inducedby}\widehat{\sigma}
. We will say that the
\textit{genus of }\widehat{\sigma}
is the cyclically ordered tuple
\left(g(\mathcal{H}_{0}),\ldots,g(\mathcal{H}_{k})\right)
, and we will denote this tuple by
g(\widehat{\sigma})
. We will prove the following result.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
be two
k
–cells of
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Then there is an
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
such that
f\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
if and only if
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
induce the same decomposition of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

We first prove the forwards implication and then we prove the backwards implication.

The forward implication
. Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq C_{[a]}(S_{g})
be 2–cells in the same
\mathcal{I}_{g}
orbit. Let
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
be an element with
f\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
. Since
f\in\Mod(S_{g})
,
f
takes any
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
to some
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
, and since
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
, we must have
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. Therefore
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
as unordered sets, so it remains to show that
f
preserves the cyclic order on
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be an oriented curve such that for any vertex
d\in\sigma
,
b
intersects
d
once with signed intersection
-1
. Then since
f(\sigma)=\sigma^{\prime}
, the image
f(b)
satisfies the property that for any
d^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\prime}
,
f(b)
intersects
cd
once with signed intersection number
-1
. Now, for each
\mathcal{H}_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
, let
c_{i}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve disjoint from
\sigma
and not homologous to
[c]
such that
c_{i}
intersects
b
once with signed intersection
-1
. An example with
\sigma
a 2–cell of the curves
\sigma,b
, and
c_{i}
can be found in Figure
4
, if we denote the vertices of
\sigma
by
d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}
.

d_{0}
c_{0}
d_{1}
c_{1}
d_{2}
c_{1}
b

Figure 4.
The curves
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
,
b
, and
c_{i}

Let
c^{\prime}_{i}=f(c_{i})
. Then
[c^{\prime}_{i}]=[c_{i}]
since
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
, and
[c^{\prime}_{i}]\in\mathcal{H}_{i}(\sigma^{\prime})
. Then the cyclic order of the elements of
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is the same as the cyclic order of the intersections of
c_{i}
with
b
, where the cyclic order on the
c_{i}
is induced by the orientation of
b
, so the lemma follows.

The backwards implication
. Suppose now that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
induce the same decomposition of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
. Reindex
\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
so that
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
0\leq i\leq\dim(\sigma)
, and let
S_{i}^{\sigma}
,
S_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
denote the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\sigma
and
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\sigma^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}
be a symplectic basis for
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
such that:

•

[a]=\alpha_{1}
and

•

there is a partition
\nu\vdash\{2,\ldots,g\}
where
\nu
has
k+1
blocks such that, for each
P_{i}\in\nu
, the set
\{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\}_{j\in P_{i}}
is a symplectic basis for a maximal unimodular subgroup of
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
.

For each
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
, let
\{a_{j},b_{j}\}_{j\in P_{i}}
and
\{a_{j}^{\prime},b_{j}^{\prime}\}_{P_{i}}
be representatives in
S_{i}^{\sigma}
and
S_{i}^{\sigma^{\prime}}
respectively for
\{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\}_{j\in P_{i}}
such that
|a_{i}\cap b_{j}|=|a^{\prime}_{i}\cap b^{\prime}_{j}|=\left|\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\rangle\right|
for any
1\leq i,j\leq g
. By the change of coordinates principle, there is an
f\in\Mod(S_{g})
taking
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
that also takes
a_{j}\rightarrow a^{\prime}_{j},b_{j}\rightarrow b_{j}^{\prime}
for all
2\leq j\leq g
. Then
f_{*}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}
and
f_{*}(\beta_{i})=\beta_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq g
. Then
f
takes a vertex of
\sigma
to a vertex of
\sigma^{\prime}
, so
f_{*}(\alpha_{1})=\alpha_{1}
. This implies that
\langle f_{*}\beta_{1},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\langle f_{*}\beta_{1},f_{*}(\beta_{i})\rangle=0
for any
2\leq i\leq g
, and
\langle\alpha_{1},f_{*}(\beta_{1})\rangle=1
. Hence
f(\beta_{1})=\beta_{1}+n\alpha_{1}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
f
is not
a priori
in the Torelli group, since it may act nontrivially on
\beta_{1}
. We now find another element
h\in\Mod(S_{g})
such that
hf\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and
hf\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
.

Let
a^{\prime}\in\sigma^{\prime}
be a curve in the
k
–cell
\sigma^{\prime}
. Then
T_{a^{\prime}}^{-n}f\sigma=T_{a^{\prime}}^{-n}\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
since
f\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and
T_{a^{\prime}}
acts trivially on
\sigma^{\prime}
by construction. Furthermore, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

T_{[a^{\prime}]}(\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1})=\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1}+\langle[a^{\prime}],\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1}\rangle[a^{\prime}].

Since
[a^{\prime}]=\alpha_{1}
, we therefore have
T_{[a^{\prime}]}(\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1})=\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1}+1\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}+(m+1)\alpha_{1}
. Therefore
T_{[a^{\prime}]}^{-n}(\beta_{1}+m\alpha_{1})=\beta_{1}+(m-n)\alpha_{1}
. Therefore we have we have
T_{[a^{\prime}]}^{-n}f_{*}(\beta_{1})=T_{[a^{\prime}]}^{-n}(\beta_{1}+n\alpha_{1})=\beta_{1}+n\alpha_{1}-n\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}
. Then
T_{a^{\prime}}
acts trivially on the set
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}\}
since
[a^{\prime}]=\alpha_{1}
trivially intersects every element in this set. Hence
(T_{a}^{-n}f)_{*}
fixes the set
\{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}\}
, so
T_{a}^{-n}f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
. Hence
T_{a}^{-n}f
is an element of the Torelli group taking
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

Given Lemma
3.1
, if
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
is a cell, the cyclically ordered decomposition
\mathcal{H}(\widehat{\sigma})
for
\widehat{\sigma}
a lift of
\sigma
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
depends only on
\sigma
, so we will use the notation
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
to refer to the decomposition of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
induced by
\widehat{\sigma}
, and refer to
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
as the
decomposition of
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
induced by
\sigma
.

3.2.
Bestvina–Margalit tori

We will now give a formal definition of a Bestvina–Margalit torus and prove a collection of results about fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

Bestvina–Margalit tori
. Let
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
be a 2–cell. There is a unique cell
\sigma^{\prime}
called the
dual cell to
\sigma
such that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
as unordered sets, but with
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)\neq\mathcal{H}(\sigma^{\prime})
as cyclically ordered sets. This
\sigma^{\prime}
is unique by Lemma
3.1
since there are exactly two cyclic orders on the set of three elements. The Bestvina–Margalit torus
\BM_{\sigma}
is the union
\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}
, and the fundamental class of
\BM_{\sigma}
is denoted
[\BM_{\sigma}]
. We have the following lemma that allows us to add fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
x,y,z
be three edges in
X_{g}
such that
\BM_{x,y}
,
\BM_{x,z}
and
\BM_{y,z}
are all Bestvina–Margalit tori. Let
yz
denote the third edge of a 2–cell
\sigma
with
y,z\subseteq\sigma
oriented so that in the group on the edges of
X_{g}
, we have
y*z=yz
. A lift of a 3–cell
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
containing these edges can be seen in Figure
5
. Then in
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, the relation
[\BM_{x,y}]+[\BM_{x,z}]=[\BM_{x,yz}]
is satisfied.

a
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}

Figure 5.
A 3–cell containing lifts of the edges
x,y,z,yz
as in Lemma
3.2
.
x
lifts to
(a,a_{1})
,
y
lifts to
(a_{1},a_{2})
,
z
lifts to
(a_{2},a_{3})
and
yz
lifts to
(a_{1},a_{3})
.

Before proving the lemma, we will explain how to compute differentials in
X_{g}
. If
\tau\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is a
k
–cell with vertices
\{c_{0},\ldots,c_{k}\}
, then the differential
\partial\tau
is given by

\partial\tau=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(c_{0},\ldots,\widehat{c_{i}},\ldots,c_{k})

where
(c_{0},\ldots,\widehat{c_{i}},\ldots,c_{k})
denotes the
(k-1)
–cell with vertices given by
c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1},c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{k}
. This differential descends to
X_{g}
by definition. Now, if
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
is a
k
–cell, Lemma
3.1
tells us that
\tau
is determined by the decomposition

\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{k}\}.

Then, if
\overline{\tau}
is a lift of
\tau
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
, forgetting a vertex
c\subseteq\overline{\tau}
, which yields a
k
–cell
\overline{\sigma}
, corresponds to replacing
\mathcal{H}_{i},\mathcal{H}_{i+1}
in
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
with
\mathcal{H}_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i+1}
. Therefore the differential
\partial\tau
is given by

\partial\tau=\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\sigma_{i}

where for
0\leq i\leq k-1
, each
\sigma_{i}
satisfies

\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{i})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{i-1},\mathcal{H}_{i}+\mathcal{H}_{i+1},\mathcal{H}_{i+2},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{k}\}.

We have that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{k})=\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{k-1},\mathcal{H}_{k}+\mathcal{H}_{0}\}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

We will find 3–dimensional subcomplex
\overline{L}\subseteq X_{g}
such that the boundary realizes the relation in the lemma. Let
\tau
be a 3–cell with
\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
. The space
\overline{L}
is given as the union of the following 3–cells:

•

\tau_{0}=\tau
,

•

\tau_{1}
that satisfies
\mathcal{H}(\tau_{1})=\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
, and

•

\tau_{2}
that satisfies
\mathcal{H}(\tau_{2})=\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
.

We can compute the differentials of each 3–cell above as follows, signed appropriately to yield the desired relation in the lemma. For notational convenience, we will conflate
\sigma
and
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
for
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
.

•

\partial\tau_{0}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
,

•

-\partial\tau_{1}=-\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
, and

•

\partial\tau_{2}=\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}\}.

Now, we see that in the sum
\partial\tau_{0}-\partial\tau_{1}+\partial\tau_{2}
, the term
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
in
\partial\tau_{0}
cancels with
-\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
in
-\partial\tau_{1}
, the term
-\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
in
\partial\tau_{0}
cancels with
\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
in
\partial\tau_{2}
, and
\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
in
-\partial\tau_{1}
cancels with
-\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
in
\partial\tau_{2}
. This means that

\displaystyle\partial\tau_{0}-\partial\tau_{1}+\partial\tau_{2}=

\displaystyle-\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{H_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}

\displaystyle-\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}

\displaystyle+\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}\}.

Now, by rearranging terms, we have

\displaystyle\partial\tau_{0}-\partial\tau_{1}+\partial\tau_{2}=

\displaystyle-\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}+\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}

\displaystyle+\{H_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}

\displaystyle+\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}-\{\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}\}.

Then each pair of two cells term on the right side of the above equation is a Bestvina–Margalit torus, so we have

\partial\tau_{0}-\partial\tau_{1}+\partial\tau_{2}=-[\BM_{x,yz}]+[\BM_{x,y}]+[\BM_{x,z}]

and therefore the relationship given in the lemma holds.
∎

We now prove Lemma
3.3
, which says when two Bestvina–Margalit tori are equal in
X_{g}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\BM_{\sigma}
and
\BM_{\tau}
be two Bestvina–Margalit tori. Then
\BM_{\sigma}=\BM_{\tau}
if and only if
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}(\tau)
as unordered sets.

Proof.

We prove both directions.

Backwards direction
. We prove the contrapositive. If
\BM_{\sigma}\neq\BM_{\tau}
, then
\sigma\neq\tau
since every cell
\sigma
has a unique dual 2–cell
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}
is a Bestvina–Margalit torus. This implies that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)\neq\mathcal{H}(\tau)
as decompositions of
[a]^{\perp}
, which implies in particular that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)\neq\mathcal{H}(\tau)
as unordered sets.

Forwards direction
. Suppose that
\BM_{\sigma}=\BM_{\tau}
. By Lemma
3.1
,
\sigma
and
\tau
are identified under the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
if and only if
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}(\tau)
as decompositions of
[a]^{\perp}
. But then if
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)\neq\mathcal{H}(\tau)
, we must have
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\mathcal{H}(\tau^{\prime})
, where
\tau^{\prime}
is the 2–cell dual to
\tau
, since there are only two cyclic orders on a set of three elements.
∎

In particular, if
\BM_{\sigma}
is a Bestvina–Margalit torus, we will use the notation
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})
to denote the decomposition
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
without the cyclic order, and we will refer to this decomposition as the
decomposition of

H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})

induced by
\BM_{\sigma}
. If
\sigma
is a 2–cell with edges
x
and
y
, we will denote the splitting
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})
by
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,y})
. We describe how
\mathcal{H}
interacts with the addition of tori in the following lemma. The idea is that if we have a 3–cell
\tau
as in Figure
5
, then we can describe the splitting induced by one torus corresponding to a 2–cell
\sigma\subseteq\tau
in terms of the splitting induced by tori corresponding to other 2–cells contained in
\tau
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
x,y,z\subseteq X_{g}
be three edges such that
\BM_{x,y}
,
\BM_{x,z}
and
\BM_{y,z}
are all Bestvina–Margalit tori. Suppose that we have

\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,y})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}\text{ and }\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,z})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{2}\}

with
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}[a]
. Suppose additionally that we have
\mathcal{H}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}(z)
. Let
yz
denote the third edge of a 2–cell
\sigma
with
y,z\subseteq\sigma
with
yz
oriented so that
y*z=yz
in the group generated by edges of
X_{g}
. Then we have

\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,yz})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{2}\}.

Proof.

We first want to compute the decomposition
\mathcal{H}(yz)
. The 2–cell
\sigma
containing
y,z
and
yz
has
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
. This implies that the third element of
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}
. Hence

\mathcal{H}(yz)=\{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp},(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp})^{\perp}\}=\{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}\}

and in particular, we have
\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{H}(yz)
. Now, the splitting
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,yz})
contains
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
, so the third element of
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{x,yz})
is given by

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}=(\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1})^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\prime},

so the lemma holds.
∎

Before moving forward with the paper, we will briefly discuss a notation for cells in
X_{g}
, as well as the action of subgroups of
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
on
X_{g}
.

Graphical representations for Bestvina–Margalit tori
. Let
x,y,z,yz\subseteq X_{g}
be a set of edges as in Lemma
3.4
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\prime}\}
be an unordered set. Then
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
corresponds to the labeled graph as in Figure
6
.

\mathcal{H}_{0}
\mathcal{H}_{1}
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{H}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\prime}

Figure 6.
The graphical representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}

The advantage of this notation is that the decompositions corresponding to
\mathcal{H}(x,y)
,
\mathcal{H}(x,z)
and
\mathcal{H}(x,yz)
can be read off from the graph. Each of these Bestvina–Margalit tori corresponds to the decomposition given by taking the span of the union of two subgroups not equal to
\mathcal{H}_{0}
. We will use this notation in Section
5
to keep track of certain decompositions of
[a]^{\perp}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
.

The action of
\Sp([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
on
X_{g}
. Throughout the rest of the paper, we will consider the action of
\Sp([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
on
X_{g}
. The subgroup
\Gamma_{a}\subseteq\Mod(S_{g})
consisting of all elements that act trivially on the class
[a]\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
acts on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. We have
\mathcal{I}_{g}\subseteq\Gamma_{a}
by definition, and so the quotient
\Gamma_{a}/\mathcal{I}_{g}=\Sp([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
acts on
X_{g}
.

4.
Abelian cycles in
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
4.1
, which says that certain subspaces of
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
generated by abelian cycles of bounding pair maps can be generated by abelian cycles supported on finitely many subsurfaces.

Abelian cycles
. Let
G
be a group and
{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}\subseteq G
be a collection of pairwise commuting elements of infinite order. Let
A=\langle c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\rangle\subseteq G
. The abelian cycle
\ab(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\in H_{k}(G;\mathbb{Q})
is the image of the class
[c_{1}]\wedge\ldots\wedge[c_{k}]\in H_{k}(A;\mathbb{Q})
under the pushforward map
H_{k}(A;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{k}(G;\mathbb{Q})
. If
k=2
, we will denote the abelian cycle
\ab(c_{1},c_{2})
by
[c_{1},c_{2}]
. If
G
is a group, we let
H_{k}^{\ab}(G;\mathbb{Q})
denote the subspace of
H_{k}(G;\mathbb{Q})
generated by abelian cycles.

Torelli group of a subsurface
. Let
S
be a compact surface and
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
be an embedding. We will denote the genus of
S
by
g(S)
. We say that
\iota
is
clean
if no connected component of
S_{g}\setminus\iota(S)
is a disk. Let
\mathcal{I}_{g}(S,S_{g})
denote the intersection
\iota_{*}(\Mod(S))\cap\mathcal{I}_{g}
, where the pushforward map
\iota_{*}
is given by extending
\varphi\in\Mod(S)
by the identity over
S_{g}\setminus\iota(S)
. Let
\mathcal{I}(\iota)
denote the inclusion map
\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{g}
.

Bounding pair maps
. If
c\subseteq S_{g}
is a curve, let
T_{c}
denote the Dehn twist along
c
. We say that
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
is a
bounding pair map
if
f=T_{c}T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}
for
c
and
c^{\prime}
two disjoint homologous curves. We will denote the bounding pair map
T_{c}T_{c^{\prime}}^{-1}
by
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
. Let
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
denote the subspace of
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
generated by abelian cycles
[T_{c,c^{\prime}},T_{d,d^{\prime}}]
such that the bounding pairs
c\cup c^{\prime}
and
d\cup d^{\prime}
are disjoint.

The main goal of the section is to prove the following result.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
M\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=9
. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. The natural map

\bigoplus_{v\in\mathcal{V}}H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}\rightarrow H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is surjective.

The idea of Proposition
4.1
is that if there are enough curves on a surface
S_{g}
, then any abelian cycle of bounding pairs is a linear combination of abelian cycles that are each disjoint from at least one of the curves.

Outline of the proof of Proposition
4.1
. If
G
is a group acting on a vector space
V
and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq G
is a subset, we let
V^{\mathcal{F}}
denote the subspace of
V
fixed by all elements of
\mathcal{F}
. If
\mathcal{W}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
, let
T_{\mathcal{W}}
denote the set of transvections along elements of
\mathcal{W}
. If
F\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
, let
[F]
denote the corresponding class in
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Let
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
be an abelian cycle. The proof of the proposition proceeds in two steps.

(1)

There is a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

with
[T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
and for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

(2)

If
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]
is an abelian cycle with
T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
, then there is a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\theta(\mathcal{V})_{j}[T_{h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

with each
[T_{h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}.

Step (1) is the content of Lemma
4.11
and Step (2) is the content of Lemma
4.9
.

Outline of the section
. We will begin by describing the Johnson homomorphism. We will then prove Lemma
4.5
, which describes the vector space
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
for
S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
a clean embedding with
g(S)\geq 3
. We then prove Lemma
4.8
which is a result about generating sets of exterior powers of quasi–unimodular lattices, and then use Lemma
4.8
to prove Lemma
4.9
. We also use Lemma
4.8
to prove Lemma
4.10
, which is another result about generating sets of exterior products of vector spaces. We use Lemma
4.10
to prove Lemma
4.11
. We then combine these lemmas to prove Proposition
4.1
.

The Johnson homomorphism
. Let
a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g}
be a symplectic basis for
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
and let
\omega=a_{1}\wedge b_{1}+\ldots+a_{g}\wedge b_{g}\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
. There is an inclusion
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
given by
[c]\rightarrow[c]\wedge\omega
. Johnson
[
11
]
constructed a map

\tau_{g}:\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})

and showed that the pushforward map

(\tau_{g})_{*}:H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is an isomorphism
[
13
]
. We will make use of Lemma
4.2
, which describes
\tau_{g}(T_{c,c^{\prime}})
for
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
a bounding pair map. The lemma is due to Johnson
[
11
, Lemma 4B]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
g\geq 3
. Let
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
be a bounding pair map and let
S
be a connected component of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(c\cup c^{\prime}\right)
. Let
a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{h},b_{h}
be a symplectic basis for a maximal nondegenerate subspace of
H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})
. We have

\tau_{g}(T_{c,c^{\prime}})=[c]\wedge\left(\sum_{i=1}^{h}a_{i}\wedge b_{i}\right).

We use Lemma
4.2
to prove Lemma
4.5
, which describes
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S;S_{g});\mathbb{Z})
for clean embeddings
S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
. We first prove an auxiliary lemma, which is essentially just a corollary of a result of Putman
[
23
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
g\geq 4
and let
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
be a clean embedding such that
g(S)\geq 3
. Then
\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g})
is generated by bounding pair maps.

Proof.

A result of Putman
[
23
, Theorem 1.3]
tells us that
\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g})
is generated by bounding pair maps and Dehn twists along separating curves. Hence it suffices to show that if
\delta\subseteq\iota(S)
is a separating curve in
S_{g}
, then
T_{\delta}
is a product of bounding pair maps. Since
g(S)\geq 3
, there is an embedded copy of
S_{1}^{2}\subseteq S
such that the image of one boundary component of
S_{1}^{2}
is the curve
\delta
. Then by embedding a copy of
S_{0}^{4}\hookrightarrow S_{1}^{2}
, we can use the lantern relation
[
8
, Section 5.1]
to rewrite
T_{\delta}
as a product of three bounding pair maps, so the lemma follows.
∎

We also need one more fact about quasi–unimodular lattices.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
L
be a quasi–unimodular lattice such that
g(L)\geq 1
. The free abelian group
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}L
is spanned by elements of the form
\gamma\wedge\delta
with
\gamma,\delta
primitive and
\langle\gamma,\delta\rangle=1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}L
be the set of all elements
\gamma\wedge\delta
as in the statement of the lemma. Let

\mathcal{B}=\{\gamma_{1},\delta_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{g(L)},\delta_{g(L)},\gamma_{g(L)+1},\gamma_{g(L)+2},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell}\}

be a partial symplectic basis for
L
. The group
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}L
is generated by all elements of the form
r\wedge s
for
r,s\in\mathcal{B}
, so it suffices to show that any element
r\wedge s
with
r,s\in\mathcal{B}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
–linear combination of elements as in the statement of the lemma.

We begin by proving this in the case
r=\gamma_{i},s=\delta_{j}
. We have
\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{j}=(\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{j})\wedge\delta_{j}-(\gamma_{j}\wedge\delta_{j})
. Each of these latter elements is a wedge of primitive elements with algebraic intersection number one, so
r\wedge s
is in the subgroup of
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}L
generated by
\mathcal{S}
.

Now, suppose that
r\in\mathcal{B},s=\delta_{i}
with
r\neq\gamma_{i}
. We have
r\wedge s=(r+\gamma_{i})\wedge\delta_{i}-\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}
, and each of these latter elements are in
\mathcal{S}
, since the only element of
\mathcal{B}
that
\delta_{i}
intersects nontrivially is
\gamma_{i}
.

Finally, suppose that
r=\gamma_{i},s=\gamma_{j}
. If
i
or
j
are less than
g(L)
, then without loss of generality we have
j\leq g(L)
. Then we have
r\wedge s=gamma_{i}+\delta_{j}\wedge\gamma_{j}-\delta_{j}\wedge\gamma_{j}
, and these latter elements are all in
\mathcal{S}
. Otherwise,
i,j>g(L)
. In this case, we have

r\wedge s=(\gamma_{i}+\delta_{g(L)})\wedge(\gamma_{j}+\gamma_{g(L)})-\delta_{g(L)}\wedge\gamma_{j}-\delta_{g(L)}\wedge\gamma_{j}-\delta_{g(L)}-\gamma_{g(L)}.

The first and less elements are in
\mathcal{S}
, and the middle two elements are
\mathbb{Z}
–linear combinations of elements in
\mathcal{S}
by the previous cases, so
r\wedge s\in\Span_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{S})
and thus the lemma holds.
∎

We now describe
H(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
for certain clean embeddings
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
S
be a compact surface with boundary with
\left|\pi_{0}(\partial S)\right|\leq 2
. Let
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
be a clean embedding such that
S_{g}{\ssearrow}S
is connected. Suppose additionally that
g(S_{g}{\ssearrow}S)\geq 1
. Then there is a surjection

f:H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\twoheadrightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})

such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge^{3}\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}}

Furthermore, suppose that
g(S)\geq 3
. Then
f
is an isomorphism, and the map
\wedge^{3}\iota_{*}\circ f
is injective.

Proof.

We begin with the first part of the lemma. We show that the image of the composition the composition

H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}}H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\iota_{*})
, and that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\iota_{*}
is an injection. If these two statements hold, then
f
is given by

f=(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\iota_{*})^{-1}|_{\im(\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}}\circ\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}.

We prove each containment in turn, and then prove injectivity.

Showing that
\im(\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*})\subseteq\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Lemma
4.3
says that
\mathcal{I}_{g}(S,S_{g})
is generated by bounding pair maps, so it suffices to show that if
T_{c,c^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}_{g}(S,S_{g})
is a bounding pair map, then
\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}(T_{c,c^{\prime}})\in\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Since the bounding pair
c\cup c^{\prime}
is contained in
S
, we have
[c]\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})
. Then there must be a connected component
S^{\prime}
of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(c\cup c^{\prime}\right)
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
. Let
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{h},\beta_{h}
be a symplectic basis for a maximal unimodular subgroup of
H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
. Lemma
4.2
says that
\tau_{g}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}([T_{c,c^{\prime}}])=[c]\wedge\left(\alpha_{1}\wedge\beta_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{h}\wedge\beta_{h}\right)\in\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
, so the
\subseteq
containment holds.

Showing that
\im(\tau_{g}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*})\supseteq\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Observe that if
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})
are primitive classes with
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=1
and
\langle v_{1},v_{3}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{3}\rangle=0
, then Lemma
4.2
says that if
c,c^{\prime}
is a bounding pair with
[c]=v_{3}
and
v_{1},v_{2}
a symplectic basis for a maximal nondegenerate subgroup of the first homology of a connected component of
S{\ssearrow}\left(c\cup c^{\prime}\right)
, then
\tau_{g}(T_{c,c^{\prime}})=v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}\in\im(\tau_{g}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*})
. Since the set of such triples of elements spans
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z})
by Lemma
4.4
, we have
\im(\tau_{g}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*})\supseteq\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
.

Showing that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})
injects into
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. We first show that the exterior power of pushforwards
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is injective. Since
S_{g}{\ssearrow}S
is connected, the pushforward
H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is injective, so
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is injective as well. Hence it suffices to show that
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\cap H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})\wedge\omega=0
, where
\omega\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the characteristic element. Suppose otherwise, so there is some nonzero
\alpha\wedge\omega\in\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Since
\omega
does not depend on the choice of symplectic basis, we can choose a symplectic basis
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}
with
\alpha\wedge\omega=m\cdot\alpha_{1}\wedge(\alpha_{1}\wedge\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}\wedge\beta_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{g}\wedge\beta_{g})=m\cdot\alpha_{1}\wedge(\alpha_{2}\wedge\beta_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{g}\wedge\beta_{g})
for
m
some positive integer. But this implies that there are
(2g-1)
linearly independent elements
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}\in\im(H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. We have assumed that
g(S_{g}{\ssearrow}S)\geq 1
, so
\dim(H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q}))\leq 2g-2
, which is a contradiction.

We now prove the second part of the lemma, where we assume that
g(S)\geq 3
. In this case, since
g(S)\geq 3
and
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
is clean, a theorem of Putman
[
25
, Theorem B]
states that the pushforward map
\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}
is an injection. Therefore the composition
(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\iota_{*})^{-1}|_{\im(\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}}\circ\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\circ\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}
is an injection. By definition this is
f
, so
f
is injective as well, and is therefore an isomorphism.
∎

We will also prove Lemma
4.7
, which is a result that describes how stabilizers of transvections interact with the abelianization of certain
\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g})
. We first prove the following result.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
g\geq 3
and let
w\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be a nonzero primitive element. The fixed space
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}}
is sent to
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
under the Johnson homomorphism.

Proof.

Since the Johnson homomorphism is a map of
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
–representations, it suffices to show that
\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)^{T_{w}}=\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Since
T_{w}
acts trivially on
[w]^{\perp}
by the definition of transvection, the transvection
T_{w}
acts trivially on
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
, and thus
\tau_{g}(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}})\supseteq\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. It therefore remains to prove the other containment.

Let
\mathcal{B}=\{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}\}\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be a symplectic basis so that
\alpha_{1}=w
. The vector space
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
has a basis consisting of all pure wedge products of elements in
\mathcal{B}
. Then after modding out by
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, we see that any pure tensor of the form
\alpha_{1}\wedge\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma
is a linear combination

\alpha_{2}\wedge\beta_{2}\wedge\gamma+\ldots+\alpha_{g}\wedge\beta_{g}\wedge\gamma.

Hence
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
has a basis consisting of all pure tensors of elements in
\mathcal{B}
except those containing
\alpha_{1}\wedge\beta_{1}
. This set is spanning in
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, and is therefore a basis by counting dimensions. Now,
T_{w}
acts trivially on any such wedge products except for those containing
\beta_{1}
. For such a basis element
\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma\wedge\delta
for
\gamma,\delta\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\{\alpha_{1}\}
, we have
T_{w}(\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma\wedge\delta)=\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma\wedge\delta+\alpha_{1}\wedge\gamma\wedge\delta
. Now, if we have a
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of such elements

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i},

then we have

T_{w}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}(\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1})\wedge\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}=(\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1})\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}.

Then to have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}
fixed by
T_{w}
, we must have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\beta_{1}\wedge\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}=(\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1})\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i},

or equivalently we must have

\alpha_{1}\wedge\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}=0

Since the span of the set
\{\gamma_{i},\delta_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
does not contain
\alpha_{1}
, we conclude that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{i}\wedge\delta_{i}=0
. In particular, this implies that only elements of the form
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
are fixed by
T_{w}
, as desired.
∎

We apply Lemma
4.6
in the following context.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
g\geq 4
, and let
\iota:S\hookrightarrow S_{g}
be a clean embedding satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma
4.5
. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve, and let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be given by
\mathcal{W}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}.
Then, after passing to
\mathbb{Q}
–coefficients, the Johnson homomorphism
\tau_{g}
restricts to an isomorphism

\im\left(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)\cap H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{W}}}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\im(H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{\perp})

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
, we have
\im\left(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)\cong\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\iota_{*})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})
. Now, the Johnson homomorphism is
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
–equivariant, so if
w\in\mathcal{W}
, the fixed set
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}}
is isomorphic to
\im(\tau_{g}\otimes\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}}
. Then by Lemma
4.6
, we have
