\tau_{g}(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}})=\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

Then we have
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{W}}}=\bigcap_{w\in\mathcal{W}}H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{w}}
and
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}=\bigcap_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}[w]^{\perp}
. Therefore we have
\tau_{g}(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{W}}})=\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Hence the intersection

\im\left(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)\cap H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{W}}}

is sent to
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\im(\tau_{g}))\cap\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\rightarrow\im(\tau_{g}))
by the Johnson homomorphism, and this last intersection is equal to

\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(\im(H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})))\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}

as desired.
∎

The remaining steps to do now before proving Proposition
4.1
are to prove Lemma
4.9
and Lemma
4.11
, which will allow us to rewrite classes
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]
as linear combinations of abelian cycles of bounding pairs maps contained in
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for various primitive
v\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
.

4.1.
The proof of Lemma
4.9

We now recall a basic fact from linear algebra.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(L,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
be quasi–unimodular lattice. Let
\mathcal{W}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{4}\}\subseteq L
be a set of primitive elements such that the image of
\mathcal{W}
under the adjoint map
L\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}(L,\mathbb{Z})
is a linearly independent set. Then the natural map

\varphi:\bigoplus_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(w^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)

is surjective.

Proof.

Choose a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
for
L\otimes\mathbb{Q}
such that
\langle w_{i},a_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
. Then the set
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\{a_{1},\ldots,\widehat{a_{i}},\ldots,a_{n}\}
is a basis for
w^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
for each
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Now, the vector space
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q})
has a basis consisting of triples of elements in
\mathcal{B}
. Since
\left|\mathcal{W}\right|\geq 4
, each of these triples lies in some
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Therefore
\im(\varphi)
contains a basis for
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(L\otimes\mathbb{Q})
and hence is surjective.
∎

For our purpose, the important takeaway from Lemma
4.8
is the following result.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
M^{\prime}\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M^{\prime})\right|\geq 4
, and let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M^{\prime})\}
. Let
d,d^{\prime}\subseteq S_{g}
be a bounding pair such that
[T_{d,d^{\prime}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
. Let
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
be an abelian cycle. There is a
\mathbb{Q}
–linear relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\lambda_{\ell}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{\ell},f_{\ell}^{\prime}}]

in
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
such that, for each
1\leq\ell\leq m
, there is a
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
such that
[T_{f_{\ell},f_{\ell}^{\prime}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
.

Proof.

Let
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
be the two connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d\cup d^{\prime}\right)
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
f,f^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
f,f^{\prime}
are in distinct connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d\cup d^{\prime}\right)
, then
f
and
f^{\prime}
are homologous to
d
, so we have a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,d}]+[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{d,f^{\prime}}]

We now have two cases.

Case 1:
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\not\subseteq\im(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z}))
. In this case, there is a
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
such that
v\in\im(H_{1}(S^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z}))
. Then
T_{f,f^{\prime}}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Z})^{T_{v}}
, so we are done.

Case 2:
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\im(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q}))
. We see that since
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is linearly independent and the elements of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
have pairwise trivial algebraic intersection, we have
g(S^{\prime})\geq 4
. Lemma
4.5
says that
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
. Lemma
4.8
implies that there is a surjection

\bigoplus_{v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}v^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q}).

Then Lemma
4.7
applied to each term on the summand on the left implies there is a surjection

\bigoplus_{v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}\cap H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q}).

Hence there is a relation in
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\lambda_{r}[F_{r}]

where each
F_{r}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Then each
F_{r}
is supported on
S^{\prime}
, so each
F_{r}
commutes with
T_{d,d^{\prime}}
. Therefore, there is a relation in
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\lambda_{r}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},F_{r}]

where each
[F_{r}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

We now show that each
[F_{r}]
can be written as a sum of classes represented by bounding pair maps. Choose an
r
with
1\leq r\leq n
and let
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
such that
[F_{r}]\in H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
. Lemma
4.7
says that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}v^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
, and this isomorphism is induced by restricting the Johnson homomorphism. Then Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.4
combine to tell us that
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}c,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
is generated by classes represented by bounding pair maps, since each element in the basis for
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}v^{\perp}\cap H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
in Lemma
4.4
is the image under
\tau_{g}
of a bounding pair map. Therefore we may rewrite
[F_{r}]
as a linear combination of classes
[T_{f_{1,r},f_{1,r}^{\prime}}],\ldots,[T_{f_{\ell_{r},r},f_{m_{r},r}^{\prime}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
. Since each
T_{f_{\ell,r},f^{\prime}_{\ell,r}}
is supported on
S^{\prime}
, each
T_{f_{\ell,r},f^{\prime}_{\ell,r}}
commutes with
T_{d,d^{\prime}}
. Therefore we have a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\lambda_{r}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_{r}}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{\ell,r},f^{\prime}_{\ell,r}}]

which by forgetting the
r
index yields a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f,f^{\prime}}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\lambda_{\ell}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{\ell},f_{\ell}^{\prime}}].

Now, since each
T_{f_{\ell},f_{\ell}^{\prime}}
is stabilized by some
T_{v}\in T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
, we have each
[T_{f_{\ell},f^{\prime}_{\ell}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

4.2.
The proof of Lemma
4.11

We begin by extending Lemma
4.8
into the following.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
L
be a quasi–unimodular lattice. Let
\mathcal{W}=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{7}\}
be a set of primitive elements in
L
such that the image of
\mathcal{W}
under the adjoint map
L\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}(L,\mathbb{Z})
is
\mathbb{Q}
–linearly independent. Then the natural map

\varphi:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}:\left|\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right|=4}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\left(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)

is surjective.

Proof.

We will prove inductively that the natural maps

\varphi_{k}:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}:\left|\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right|=k}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\left(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(L\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right)

are surjective for
k\leq 4
.

Base case:
k=1
. This follows by applying Lemma
4.8
with any subset
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
with
\left|\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right|=4
.

Inductive step:
2\leq k\leq 4
. Assume that the map
\varphi_{k^{\prime}}
is surjective for all
1\leq k^{\prime}<k
. We will show that
\varphi_{k}
is surjective as well. Since
\varphi_{k^{\prime}}
is surjective, it suffices to show that, for any
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
with
\left|\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right|=k-1
, we have

\im(\varphi_{k})\supseteq\im\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}L\otimes\mathbb{Q}\right).

Since
k\leq 4
and
\left|\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right|<k
, we have
\left|\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right|\geq 7-k>7-4=3
, so
\left|\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right|\geq 4
. Hence the natural map

\varphi_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}}:\bigoplus_{w\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\cup\{w\}\right)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\right)^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is surjective by Lemma
4.8
. But then
\im(\varphi_{k^{\prime}}\circ\varphi_{\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}})\subseteq\im(\varphi_{k})
for any
\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{W}
, so
\im(\varphi_{k^{\prime}})\subseteq\im(\varphi_{k})
. Since
\im(\varphi_{k^{\prime}})=\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}L\otimes\mathbb{Q}
by the inductive hypothesis, the proof is complete.
∎

We use Lemma
4.10
to prove the following result about
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
be an abelian cycle. Assume that one connected component of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d\cup d^{\prime}\right)
has genus one. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=9
and let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Then there is a linear relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

with each
T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4.

Proof.

The proof follows the same approach as Lemma
4.9
. Let
S\sqcup S=S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d\cup d^{\prime}\right)
with
g(S)=1
. Since
e\cup e^{\prime}
is disjoint from
d\cup d^{\prime}
, we have
e,e^{\prime}\subseteq S_{1}
. Since
g(S)=1
, we must have
e,e^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Now, choose a primitive element
[b]\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
such that
[b]\in[e]^{\perp}
and
\langle[b],[d]\rangle=1
. Since
g(S)=1
and
\langle[b],[d]\rangle=1
, we have
g(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cap[b]^{\perp})=g-2
. Then since the elements of
\mathcal{V}
have pairwise trivial algebraic intersection, the projection of
\mathcal{V}
to
H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cap[b]^{\perp}
must contain a set of at least 7 elements whose image under the adjoint map
H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cap[b]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})
is
\mathbb{Q}
–linearly independent. Let
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
be the image of the projection of
\mathcal{V}
to
H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cap[b]^{\perp}
. Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{V}}
be a set of at least 7 linearly independent elements. By applying Lemma
4.10
to
H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathcal{B}
, there is a surjection

\bigoplus_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}:\left|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\right|=4}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q}).

Let
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}
be the preimage of
\mathcal{B}
under the projection map
\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}
. Since for any
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we have
v^{\perp}\cap H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})=\left(\proj_{H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\cap[b]^{\perp}}v\right)^{\perp}\cap H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
, we therefore have a surjection

\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}:\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\cap H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q}).

By construction, the complement
S_{g}{\ssearrow}S^{\prime}
is connected and has
g(S_{g}{\ssearrow}S^{\prime})\geq 1
. Hence by Lemma
4.5
, there is an isomorphism
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
. Furthermore, by applying Lemma
4.7
, we have
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\cap H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
. Then, since
T_{e,e^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
, we have a relation in
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[F_{i}]

where each
[F_{i}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}
with
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
. Since any
f\in\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
commutes with
T_{d,d^{\prime}}
, we have a relation in
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},F_{i}]

where each
[F_{i}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
with
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
. By Lemma
4.7
, we have an isomorphism
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\im(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})^{\perp}
. Then Lemma
4.4
gives us a spanning set for the vector space
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\im(H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})^{\perp}
, and by Lemma
4.2
each element of this spanning set is given by applying
\tau_{g}
to a bounding pair map supported on a bounding pair
f\cup f^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Hence there is a relation in
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

such that each
T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
, so the lemma holds.
∎

We will need one more auxiliary result, which well help us apply Lemma
4.11
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Then there is a linear relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{j=1}^{r}[T_{d_{j},d^{\prime}_{j}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]

in
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
such that , for each
1\leq j\leq r
, at least one connected component of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d_{j}\cup d^{\prime}_{j}\right)
has genus one.

Proof.

Let
S\cup S^{\prime}
be the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}(d\cup d^{\prime})
. If
e,e^{\prime}
are contained in different connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(d\cup d^{\prime}\right)
, we have a relation
T_{e,e^{\prime}}=T_{e,d}T_{d,e^{\prime}}
. Hence there is a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,d}]+[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{d,e^{\prime}}]

in
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore without loss of generality, we may assume that
e,e^{\prime}\subseteq S
. Now, let
d_{0},\ldots,d_{k}\subseteq S^{\prime}
be curves such that the following hold:

•

d_{0}=d
,

•

d_{k}=d^{\prime}
,

•

d_{i}
and
d_{j}
are disjoint for all
0\leq i<j\leq k
,

•

d_{i}
is homologous to
d
for all
0\leq i\leq k
, and

•

the connected components of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}\left(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k}d_{i}\right)
all have genus one.

By construction, we have
T_{d,d^{\prime}}=\prod_{0\leq i\leq k-1}T_{d_{i},d_{i+1}}
. Furthermore, each bounding pair
d_{i}\cup d_{i+1}
is contained in
S^{\prime}
, and hence each
T_{d_{i},d_{i+1}}
commutes with
T_{e,e^{\prime}}
. Therefore there is a relation in
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}[T_{d_{i},d_{i+1}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}],

so the lemma holds.
∎

We now conclude Section
4
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Let
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
be an abelian cycle. The proof of the proposition proceeds in two steps.

(1)

There is a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

with each
[T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
with
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4.

(2)

If
[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]
is an abelian cycle with
T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
with
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
, then there is a relation

[T_{d,d^{\prime}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}[T_{h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}},T_{f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}}]

with each
[T_{h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{v}}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}.

Lemma
4.12
says that we may assume without loss of generality that at least one connected component of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}(d\cup d^{\prime})
has genus one. Then Step (1) is the content of Lemma
4.11
and Step (2) is the content of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

5.
Finiteness of coinvariants in
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})

The main goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating curve. Let
[b]\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be a homology class with
\langle[a],[b]\rangle=1
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a representative of
[b]
with geometric intersection
\left|a\cap b\right|=1
. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup b)
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=9
. Let
G
be the image of the map
\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(a\cup b\cup M\right))\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
. The vector space

H_{0}(G;\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))

is finite dimensional.

The outline of Section
5
. Let
g\geq 51
and
a,b,M
be as in the statement of Lemma
5.1
and let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. We will begin by describing some algebraic invariants of Bestvina–Margalit tori, which record how the elements in
\mathcal{V}
project onto the different subgroups in
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})
. We will then prove Lemma
5.2
, which allows us to use these algebraic invariants to determine when two Bestvina–Margalit tori
\BM_{\sigma}
and
\BM_{\tau}
are in the same
G
–orbit for certain subgroups
G\subseteq\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
. We will then prove Lemma
5.3
, which describes how these algebraic invariants interact with addition of fundamental classes. We will then prove Lemma
5.5
and Lemma
5.7
. These two results will allow decompose a class
[\BM_{\sigma}]
into “simpler” classes, where “simpler” means roughly that the algebraic invariants of the torus
\BM_{\sigma}
are bounded. These last two lemmas make up the main work of the section.

Algebraic invariants of Bestvina–Margalit tori
. We begin by describing the invariants of Bestvina–Margalit tori that we will use to prove Lemma
5.1
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}\}
as in Section
3
. For each
v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, let
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})
denote the maximal
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that that
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=mw
for some nonzero
w\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}
. For each
v_{i},v_{j}\in\mathcal{V}
, let

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=\langle\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i}),\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{j})\rangle.

We now prove a result that describes the orbits of Bestvina–Margalit tori under the action of stabilizer subgroups of
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
M\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating multicurve disjoint from
a
and
b
. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
, and assume that the elements of
\mathcal{V}
are indexed as
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
. Let
G
be the group
\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(a\cup b\cup M\right))\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. Let
\sigma,\tau\subseteq X_{g}
be 2–cells and let
\BM_{\sigma},\BM_{\tau}
be the corresponding Bestvina–Margalit tori. The tori
\BM_{\sigma}
and
\BM_{\tau}
are in the same
G
–orbit if, after possibly reindexing
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\tau})
, we have:

•

g(\sigma)=g(\tau)
,

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau})
for
1\leq i\leq n,0\leq k\leq 2,
and

•

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\tau)
for
1\leq i,j\leq n,0\leq k\leq 2.

Proof.

Let
\BM_{\sigma},\BM_{\tau}
be two Bestvina–Margalit tori that satisfy the hypotheses of the lemma. Because
g(\sigma)=g(\tau)
, there is an
f\in\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}([a],[b])
satisfying
f\BM_{\sigma}=\BM_{\tau}
. Therefore, after possibly reindexing, we have
f(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma})=\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\tau}
for every
0\leq k\leq 2
.

We claim that
f
sends
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})
to
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\tau}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})
for every
i,k
. Indeed, such an element exists since
g(\mathcal{H}(\sigma))=g(\mathcal{H}(\tau))
and
\BM_{\sigma}
and
\BM_{\tau}
have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau})
and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\tau)
. Hence for each
\mathcal{H}_{k}(\tau)
there is an
f_{k}\in\Stab_{\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})}([a],[b])
which is the identity on
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\prime}}(\tau)
for
k^{\prime}\neq k
and which takes
f(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})
to
v_{i,k}^{\tau}
. Then we can replace
f
with
f_{0}f_{1}f_{2}f
, which satisfies
f\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\tau}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})
. But then this new
f
fixes every
v_{i}
, since

f(v_{i})=\sum_{k\in\{1,2,3\}}f(\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i}))=\sum_{k\in\{1,2,3\}}\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\tau}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=v_{i}.

It now remains to show that
f\in G
, i.e., that there is some
F\in\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup b\cup M))
such that the image of
F
under the symplectic representation is
f
. Since the symplectic representation
\Mod(S_{g})\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
is surjective, there is some
F^{\prime}\in\Mod(S_{g})
such that
F^{\prime}
is sent to
f
by the symplectic representation. Since
\mathcal{V}
can be represented by
M
disjoint from
a
and
b
, we can extend
\mathcal{V}\cup\{a,b\}
to a symplectic basis
\mathcal{B}=\{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}\}
. Choose a set of curves
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
such that:

•

\pi_{0}(M),\{a,b\}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
,

•

\mathcal{B}=\{[c]:c\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\}
, and

•

\left|c\cap c^{\prime}\right|=\left|\langle[c],[c^{\prime}]\rangle\right|
for all
c,c^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
be another set of representatives for
\mathcal{B}
satisfying the above conditions, except we have

\pi_{0}(F^{\prime}(M)),\{F^{\prime}(a),F^{\prime}(b)\}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}.

But now by the change of coordinates principle, there is some
F^{\prime\prime}\in\Mod(S_{g})
taking
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
in such a way that
F^{\prime\prime}_{*}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{B}
. But then
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
, so the image of
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}
under the symplectic representation is the same as
F^{\prime}
, which is
f
. Then we have
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}(M)=M
,
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}(a)=a
, and
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}(b)=b
. Hence
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}\in\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}(M\cup a\cup b))
and
F^{\prime\prime}\cdot F^{\prime}
maps to
f
under the symplectic representation, so
f\in G
, as desired.
∎

We now prove a lemma that describes how the
\theta(\mathcal{V})
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}
interact with the Bestvina–Margalit tori configured as in Lemma
5.3
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a,b,M\subseteq S_{g}
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be as in Lemma
5.2
. Let
x,y,z\subseteq X_{g}
and
\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{2}
be as in Lemma
3.4
. For any choice of
*=y,z
, and
yz
, let
\sigma_{*}
denote the 2–cell containing
x
and
*
. Let
v_{i,k}^{*}
denote
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{*})\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})
, and let
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(*)
denote
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma_{*})
. Then the following equalities hold for all
1\leq i,j\leq 9
:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{y})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{z})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{yz})
,

(2)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{yz})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))
,

(3)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{y})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{yz}))
,

(4)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{z})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{yz}))
,

(5)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(y)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(z)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(yz)
,

(6)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(y)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(z)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(yz)
,

(7)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(z)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(yz)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(y)
, and

(8)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(y)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(yz)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(z)
.

Proof.

Since we have chosen the multicurve
M
to be disjoint from
a
and
b
, we have
\mathcal{V}\subseteq[b]^{\perp}
. Then, if
X,Y\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
are two quasi–unimodular lattices with
X\cap Y=\mathbb{Z}[a]
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(X)=2g(X)+1
,
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(Y)=2g(Y)+1
, we see that for any
v\in[a]^{\perp}\cap[b]^{\perp}
, we have

\proj_{(X+Y)\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v)=\proj_{X\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v)+\proj_{Y\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v).

Now, Lemma
3.4
says that the following hold:

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{yz}
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{yz}
,

(3)

\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}+\mathcal{H}_{2}^{yz}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{z}
, and

(4)

\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}+\mathcal{H}_{2}^{yz}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{y}
.

Hence the above observation about projections tells us that the following equalities among
v_{i,k}^{y}
,
v_{i,k}^{z}
and
v_{i,k}^{yz}
hold for all
1\leq i\leq 9
:

(a)

v_{i,0}^{y}=v_{i,0}^{z}=v_{i,0}^{yz}
,

(b)

v_{i,1}^{y}+v_{i,1}^{z}=v_{i,1}^{yz}
,

(c)

v_{i,1}^{y}+v_{i,2}^{yz}=v_{i,2}^{z}
, and

(d)

v_{i,1}^{z}+v_{i,2}^{yz}=v_{i,2}^{y}
.

The relations in the statement of the lemma are derived as follows.

Relations (1) and (5)
. Since
v_{i,0}^{y}=v_{i,0}^{z}=v_{i,0}^{yz}
by equality (a), we must have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{y})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{z})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{yz})
, and similarly for
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(y)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(z)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(yz).

Relation (2) and (6)
. We have
v_{i,1}^{y}+v_{i,1}^{z}=v_{i,1}^{yz}
by relation (b). If
w_{i,1}^{*}
is a primitive class with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{*})w_{i,1}^{*}=v_{i,1}^{*}
for some choice of
*=y,z,yz
, then by relation (b) we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y})w_{i,1}^{y}+\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z})w_{i,1}^{z}=v_{i,1}^{yz}
. By Lemma
3.4
, the group
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x,yz}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{yz})
is given by
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x,y}+\mathcal{H}^{x,z}_{1}
with
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x,y}\cap\mathcal{H}^{x,z}_{1}=\mathbb{Z}[a]
, so we may represent
w_{i,1}^{y}
and
w_{i,1}^{z}
using disjoint curves
c_{y},c_{z}\subseteq S_{g}
. Then the homology class

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y})/\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))\cdot[c_{y}]+\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z})/\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))\cdot[c_{z}]

is primitive, since by definition
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y})/\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z})/\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))
are relatively prime. Therefore
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{yz})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{y}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{z}))
. For relation (6), relation (b) implies that

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}^{yz}=\langle v_{i,1}^{y}+v_{i,1}^{z},v_{j,1}^{y}+v_{j,1}^{z}\rangle.

As above,
v_{i,1}^{y}
and
v_{j,1}^{z}
, can be represented by multiples of disjoint curves, and similarly for
v_{j,1}^{z}
and
v_{i,1}^{y}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}^{yz}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{i,1}^{y},v_{j,1}^{y}\rangle+\langle v_{i,1}^{y},v_{j,1}^{z}\rangle+\langle v_{i,1}^{z},v_{j,1}^{y}\rangle+\langle v_{i,1}^{z},v_{j,1}^{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{i,1}^{y},v_{j,1}^{y}\rangle+\langle v_{i,1}^{z},v_{j,1}^{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(y)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(z).

Relations (3) and (7)
. These two relations follow from relation (c) using an argument similar to that for relations (2) and (6).

Relations (4) and (8)
. These two relations follow from relation (d) using an argument to that for relations (2) and (6).
∎

5.1.
The proof of Lemma
5.5

We begin by proving an auxiliary result, which we will need to prove Lemma
5.5
and Lemma
5.7
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a,b,M\subseteq S_{g}
, and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be as in Lemma
5.2
. Let
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
be a 2–cell. Suppose that there is a
1\leq m\leq 9
such that the following hold:

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
,

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})\geq 2
,

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})=\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma}):0\leq k\leq 2\}
, and

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,1}^{\sigma})\neq 0,\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
.

Then there is a relation in
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[\BM_{\sigma}]=\sum_{\iota=1}^{q}\lambda_{\iota}[\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}]

such that, for all
1\leq\iota\leq q
:

(1)

we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
,

(2)

the set
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}}):0\leq k\leq 2\}
is upper bounded by
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
,

(3)

the set
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}}):0\leq k\leq 2\}
has no more elements equal to
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma_{\iota}})
than the does the set
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma}):0\leq k\leq 2\}
,

(4)

if
\max_{0\leq k\leq 2}\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\}=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
, we have
0<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})<\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}}):0\leq k\leq 2\}
, and

(5)

we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\geq 10
.

Proof.

If
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
, then we are done by taking
q=1
,
\lambda_{1}=1
and
[\BM_{\sigma_{1}}]=[\BM_{\sigma}]
. Otherwise, since
g\geq 51
, there is a
\kappa=0,2
such that
g(\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma})\geq 19
. Then since
\left|\mathcal{V}\right|=9
, the fact that
g(\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma})\geq 19
implies that there is a primitive
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma}
such that the following hold:

•

a\in\mathcal{H}
,

•

v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
,

•

g(\mathcal{H})=9
,

•

g(\mathcal{H})+g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
, and

•

2*g(\mathcal{H})+1=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H})
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}\}
. We can describe
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
using the graphical notation of Section
3
as in Figure
7
.

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}

Figure 7.
The graphical representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}

Let
z\subseteq X_{g}
be the edge with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(z)
. Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
the edge with
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be the edge with
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
, where
0\leq\kappa^{\prime}\leq 2
and
\kappa^{\prime}\neq 1,\kappa
. After orienting
y
and
z
correctly, we let
yz
denote the third edge of a 2–cell in
X_{g}
containing
y
and
z
. We have
\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(yz)
, so
x
and
yz
are two edges of the 2–cell
\sigma
. Now, Lemma
3.2
says that there is a relation

[\BM_{x,yz}]=[\BM_{x,y}]+[\BM_{x,z}].

Now, let
\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z}\subseteq X_{g}
be 2–cells containing
x
and then
y
and
z
respectively. We will now show that
\sigma_{z}
and
\sigma_{y}
desired properties for
\sigma_{\iota}
.

The 2–cell
\sigma_{z}
satisfies the desired properties of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{z})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma_{z}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma}
. By our assumption that
v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
, the following hold:

(1)

v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma_{z}}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
,

(2)

v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}}=v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}+v_{i,1}^{\sigma}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
, and

(3)

v_{i,\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{z}}=v_{i,\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
.

Then we can compute
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma_{z}})
as follows.

(1)

For
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, we have
v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}}
primitive. The class
v_{i,k}^{\sigma}
is primitive for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
by hypothesis, so
v_{i,\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{z}}
is primitive. Additionally, for each
1\leq i<m
, the class
v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}
can be represented by a simple closed curve disjoint from a representative for the class
v_{i,1}^{\sigma}
, so
v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}}
is primitive. Hence
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}})\leq 1
for
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
.

(2)

For
i=m
,
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma_{z}})=0
,
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{z}})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{z}})
, and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma}))
.

The computation of
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}})
above implies that
\sigma_{z}
satisfies property (1) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Then the computation of
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma_{z}})
implies that
\sigma_{z}
satisfies properties (2)–(4) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Indeed, properties (2) and (3) follow from the fact that
\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma}))\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma})
. Property (4) follows from the fact that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,\kappa}^{\sigma}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma}))\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma})<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma})
. Finally, property (5) follows from the fact that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})+g(\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma})-g(\mathcal{H})
. Indeed, we have assumed that
g(\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma})\geq 19
, so
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}})\geq 19-g(\mathcal{H})
. Then since
g(\mathcal{H})=9
by hypothesis, we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}})\geq 19-9\geq 10
, so
\sigma_{z}
satisfies property (5) of
\sigma_{\iota}
.

The 2–cell
\sigma_{y}
satisfies the desired properties of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{y})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}+\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}^{\sigma_{y}}
. The assumption that
v_{i,1}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
implies that
v_{i,k}^{\sigma}=v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{y}}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
,
0\leq k\leq 2
. Therefore since
\sigma
satisfies properties (1)–(4) of
\sigma_{\iota}
by hypothesis, then
\sigma_{\iota}
does as well. Then
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})+g(\mathcal{H})\geq 1+9=10
by assumption, so
\sigma_{y}
satisfies property (5) of
\sigma_{\iota}
.

Now, we have shown that
[\BM_{x,yz}]=[\BM_{x,y}]+[\BM_{x,z}]
. By our choice of
x
and
y
, we have
\BM_{x,yz}=\BM_{\sigma}
. Therefore we have a relation

[\BM_{x,y}]=[\BM_{x,z}]-[\BM_{x,yz}].

By taking
s=2
,
\tau_{1}=\sigma_{z}
,
\tau_{2}=\sigma_{yz}
,
\lambda_{1}=1
, and
\lambda_{2}=-1
, the proof is complete.
∎

We now prove Lemma
5.5
, which says that any fundamental class
[\BM_{\sigma}]
is a linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]
with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell}})\leq 1.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a,b,M\subseteq S_{g}
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be as in Lemma
5.2
. Let
\BM_{\sigma}
be a Bestvina–Margalit torus. Then there is a collection of 2–cells
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{p}\subseteq X_{g}
that satisfy

[\BM_{\sigma}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]

and such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell}})\leq 1
for every
1\leq\ell\leq p
,
1\leq i\leq 9
and
k=0,1,2
.

Proof.

We induct on the number
m
with
0\leq m\leq 9
such that we can write
[\BM_{\sigma}]
as a linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]
that satisfy, for
1\leq\ell\leq p
and
1\leq i\leq m
, the inequality
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{s}})\leq 1
.

Base case:
m=0
. In this case, the result holds trivially, since there are no
i
with
1\leq i
and
i\leq m
.

Inductive step:
m\geq 1
. Inductively, assume that
[\BM_{\sigma}]
can be written as a linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]
that satisfy, for
1\leq\ell\leq p
and
1\leq i\leq m
, the inequality
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{s}})\leq 1
. Hence without loss of generality, we may assume that
\BM_{\sigma}
satisfies
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for
1\leq i<m
. We will show that
[\BM_{\sigma}]
is a linear combination of fundamental classes
[\BM_{\tau}]
, each of which has
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. We will perform a double induction on two quantities associated to
\sigma
:

•

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)=\max_{0\leq k\leq 2}\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})\}
, and

•

\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)=\left|\{k:0\leq k\leq 2,\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})=\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)\}\right|
.

In particular, we suppose that
\sigma
is a 2–cell with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. We suppose that for each 2–cell
\tau
with:

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau})\leq 1
for
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, and either:

–

\maxrk_{m}(\tau)<\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
, or

–

\maxrk_{m}(\tau)\leq\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\tau)<\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
,

we know that
[\BM_{\tau}]
is a
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]
such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. We will show that this implies that the same holds for
[\BM_{\sigma}]
.

Base case:
\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)=1
. In this case,
\BM_{\sigma}
satisfies
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, so the inductive step for the induction on
m
is complete.

Inductive step for
\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
: The inductive hypothesis for the induction on
m
holds for all
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
with:

•

either
\maxrk_{m}(\tau)<\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
or

•

both
\maxrk_{m}(\tau)\leq\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\tau)<\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
.

We will show that

[\BM_{\sigma}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]

such that the following hold:

•

at least one of the following holds:

–

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})<\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
or

–

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})<\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
,

•

\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Q}
, and

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
k=0,1,2
.

This completes the proof, since every
\tau_{\ell}
as above satisfies the inductive hypothesis for the induction on
m
by the inductive hypothesis for the induction on
\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
.

If
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})=0
for two distinct choices of
k
, then
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})=1
for the third choice of
k
, since
v_{m}=v_{m,0}^{\sigma}+v_{m,1}^{\sigma}+v_{m,2}^{\sigma}
and
v_{m}
is primitive by assumption. Otherwise, reindex
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})
such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
is maximal among all
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})
. Note that at least one remaining
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,i}^{\sigma})
must have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,i}^{\sigma})\neq 0,\neq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
since
v_{m}
is primitive by assumption. Hence, we may further reindex so that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,1}^{\sigma})\neq 0,\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
. Then
\sigma
satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
5.4
, so the conclusion of Lemma
5.4
implies that we may rewrite
[\BM_{\sigma}]
as a linear combination of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori such that each tori
\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}
satisfies the following:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
,

(2)

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
,

(3)

if
\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})=\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
, then
\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)
,

(4)

if
\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{\iota})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
, then
0<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma_{\iota}})
, and

(5)

g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\geq 10
.

Therefore we may assume without loss of generality that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
.

Now, since
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
and
\left|\mathcal{V}\right|\leq 9
, there must be some nonzero primitive
h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}\cap[b]^{\perp}
such that there is a with multicurve
M_{1}\subseteq S_{g}
where
\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M_{1})\}=\{h,w_{1,1}^{\sigma},\ldots,w_{9,1}^{\sigma}\}
, where
w_{i,1}^{\sigma}
is a primitive element with
v_{i,1}^{\sigma}=\lambda w_{i,1}^{\sigma}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}
. For
1\leq i\leq 9
, let
h_{i}=v_{i,1}^{\sigma}-h
. Since
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
, there is a primitive subgroup
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
such that the following hold:

•

a\in\mathcal{H}
,

•

2g(\mathcal{H})+1=\rk(\mathcal{H})
,

•

h_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
, and

•

h\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\perp},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}\}
, which can be graphically represented as in Figure
8
.

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}

Figure 8.
The graphical representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}

Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge such that
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
. Let
x
be the edge of
\sigma
with
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
. There is a unique edge
z\subseteq X_{g}
such that:

•

y
and
z
are two edges in a 2–cell
\tau
and

•

the third edge of
\tau
, denoted
yz
, is the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(yz)
.

Let
\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
denote 2–cells with
x,y\subseteq\sigma_{y}
,
x,z\subseteq\sigma_{z}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{y})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}+\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}
. Our choice of
\mathcal{H}
implies that
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=h_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
. Since
h
is nonzero and primitive by assumption, the element
h_{i}
is nonzero and primitive as well for an
1\leq i\leq 9
, so
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{y}})=1
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
. Assume now that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{z})
is indexed such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}
. This means that
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=h
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
, so
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}})=1
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
, since
h
is primitive by assumption. Furthermore, for such
y
and
z
, after possibly reorienting
\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
, we have

[\BM_{\sigma}]=[\BM_{\sigma_{y}}]+[\BM_{\sigma_{z}}].

We have assumed that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{y})
and
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{z})
are indexed so that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{z}}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}.
Relations (1)–(4) of Lemma
5.3
and the above computations of
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{y}})
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}})
imply that the following hold for all
1\leq i\leq 9
:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{\sigma_{y}})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{\sigma_{z}})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,0}^{\sigma})
,

(2)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{y}}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}}))=1
,

(3)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma_{y}})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{z}}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma}))=\gcd(1,\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma}))=1
, and

(4)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma_{z}})=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,1}^{\sigma_{y}}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma}))=\gcd(1,\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,2}^{\sigma}))=1
.

We have assumed that
1\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,1}^{\sigma})<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
, so we must have:

•

1=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma_{y}})<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
and

•

1=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma_{z}})<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
.

Then relations (1)–(4) together imply the following:

•

For any pair
i,k
with
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
, we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{y}}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}})\leq 1
, and

•

for any pair
i,k
with
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma}\geq 1
, we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{y}}),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}})\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})
.

Therefore, since we have assumed that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
was maximal over all
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,k}^{\sigma})
, we see that the two sets
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{y}})\}_{1\leq i\leq m,k=0,1,2}
and
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}})\}_{1\leq i\leq m,k=0,1,2}
each have strictly fewer elements equal to or exceeding
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{m,2}^{\sigma})
than does the set
\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\}_{1\leq i\leq m,k=0,1,2}
. Hence for
y
, we have:

•

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{y}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, and

•

either:

–

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{y})<\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
, or

–

\maxrk_{m}(\sigma_{y})\leq\maxrk_{m}(\sigma)
and
\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma_{y})<\nummaxrk_{m}(\sigma)

and similarly for
z
. Therefore we have

[\BM_{\sigma}]=[\BM_{\sigma_{y}}]+[\BM_{\sigma_{z}}]

such that
\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
satisfy the inductive hypothesis for the induction on
\maxrk_{m}
and
\nummaxrk_{m}
. The inductive hypothesis for
\maxrk_{m}
and
\nummaxrk_{m}
says that there are relations

[\BM_{\sigma_{y}}]=\sum_{\ell_{y}=1}^{p_{y}}\lambda_{\ell_{y}}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell_{y}}}]\text{ and }[\BM_{\sigma_{z}}]=\sum_{\ell_{z}=1}^{p_{z}}\lambda_{\ell_{z}}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell_{z}}}]

such that for each
1\leq\ell_{y}\leq p_{y}
and for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
0\leq k\leq 2
, we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell_{y}}})\leq 1
, and similarly for
z
. By combining these two relations, we have

[\BM_{\sigma}]=\sum_{\ell_{y}=1}^{p_{y}}\lambda_{\ell_{y}}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell_{y}}}]+\sum_{\ell_{z}=1}^{p_{z}}\lambda_{\ell_{z}}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell_{z}}}]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\lambda_{\ell}[\BM_{\tau_{\ell}}]

where for each
1\leq\ell\leq p
and
1\leq i\leq m
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{\ell}})\leq 1
, so the inductive step is complete.
∎

5.2.
The proof of Lemma
5.7

We are now almost ready to show that fundamental classes
[\BM_{\sigma}]
with
\left|\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\right|\leq 1
can be written as linear combinations of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{s}}]
with
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\tau_{s})\right|\leq 1
. We first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a,b,M\subseteq S_{g}
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be as in Lemma
5.2
. Let
\BM_{\sigma}\subseteq X_{g}
be a Bestvina–Margalit torus such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for every
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. Assume that not all pairs
1\leq i,j\leq 9
satisfy
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|\leq 1
. Choose a pair
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
with
1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq 9
such that, after possibly reindexing
\mathcal{H}(\BM_{\sigma})
,
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|\geq 2
and
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
is maximal in the set
\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma)\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\right\}
. Then
\BM_{\sigma}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}]
for
1\leq\iota\leq s
such that the following hold for all
1\leq\iota\leq s
:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
,

(2)

the set
\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\right\}
is bounded above by
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
and has no more elements equal to
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
than does the set
\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma)\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\right\}
,

(3)

if the set
\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\}
has as many elements equal to
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
as does the set
\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma)\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\}
, then
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
,

(4)

for every pair
1\leq i<j\leq 9
with
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|\leq 1
for every
0\leq k\leq 2
, we have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|\leq 1
for all
0\leq k\leq 2
, and

(5)

g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\geq 11
.

Proof.

If
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 11
, then the lemma is trivially true by taking
s=1
and
\BM_{\sigma_{1}}=\BM_{\sigma}
. Otherwise, since
g\geq 51
there is a
k=0,2
with
g(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\sigma})\geq 21
. Without loss of generality, assume that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma})\geq 21
. Since
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma})\geq 21
and
\left|\mathcal{V}\right|=9
, there is a primitive subgroup
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
such that the following hold:

•

a\in\mathcal{H}
,

•

v_{i,k}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
,

•

g(\mathcal{H})-g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 9
,

•

g(\mathcal{H})\geq 11
, and

•

2*g(\mathcal{H})+1=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H})
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}\}
. We can describe
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
using the graphical notation of Section
3
as in Figure
9
.

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}

Figure 9.
The graphical representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}

Now, let
z\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(z)
. Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
. Reorient
y
and
z
so that
yz
is the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(yz)
. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
. For
*=y,z
, let
\sigma_{*}\subseteq X_{g}
be a 2–cell containing
x
and
*
. By Lemma
3.2
, there is a relation

[\BM_{\sigma}]=[\BM_{\sigma_{y}}]+[\BM_{\sigma_{z}}].

We now show that
\BM_{\sigma_{y}}
and
\BM_{\sigma_{z}}
have the desired properties of the tori
\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}
.

\BM_{\sigma_{z}}
satisfies properties (1)-(5) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{z})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{z}}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{z}}
. Now, we have chosen
\mathcal{H}
such that
\proj_{\mathcal{H}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=0
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
. Furthermore, for
k=1
, we have
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=v_{i,0}^{\sigma}+v_{i,1}^{\sigma}
. For
k=2
, we have
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{z}}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=v_{i,2}^{\sigma}
. Each
v_{i,k}^{\sigma}
is primitive by our hypothesis that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
, so each
v_{i,k}^{\sigma_{z}}
is primitive as well. Therefore
\BM_{\sigma_{z}}
satisfies property (1) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Furthermore, the following hold for
\sigma_{z}
:

(1)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(\sigma_{z})=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 9
,

(2)

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma_{z})\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(\sigma)\right|
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 9
,

(3)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma_{z})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 9
, and

(4)

g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})+g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma})-g(\mathcal{H})
.

Now, observe that since
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=0
, if
1\leq i<j\leq 9
is a pair such that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\in\{-1,0,1\}
for every
0\leq k\leq 2
, then either
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=0
for
0\leq k\leq 2
, or
\{\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma):0\leq k\leq 2\}=\{-1,0,1\}
. Therefore, relations (1), (2) and (3) in the above list for relations among
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma_{z})
imply that
\BM_{\sigma_{z}}
satisfies property (4) of the lemma. For property (2), observe that the fact that
\langle v_{i^{\prime}},v_{j^{\prime}}\rangle=0
implies that

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0}(\sigma)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2}(\sigma)=0

Since
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
is maximal among
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma)\right|
, the integers
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)
and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0}(\sigma)
must have opposite signs. Therefore
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma_{z})\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)+\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|\leq\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
, so relations (1), (2), and (3) imply that
\BM_{\sigma_{z}}
satisfies property (2) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. For property (3), relations (1)-(3) imply that the only way for the set
\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma_{z})\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\right\}
to have as many elements equal to
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
as does
\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma)\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\right\}
is for
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma_{z})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)
, so property (3) must hold. Then since
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma})-g(\mathcal{H})\geq 21-11=10
by assumption, relation (4) in the list of relations above implies that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{z}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma})-g(\mathcal{H})+g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10+1\geq 11
, so
\sigma_{z}
satisfies property (5) of
\sigma_{\iota}
.

\BM_{\sigma_{y}}
satisfies hypothesis (1)-(5) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{y})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma_{y}}
,
\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}
. We have
\proj_{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}(\sigma)\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=v_{i,0}^{\sigma}
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
by our choice of
\mathcal{H}
. Since we have
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma}
, our choice of
\mathcal{H}
implies that
\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=v_{i,1}^{\sigma}
. Then we also have
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma_{y}}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}
by construction, so the following hold:

(1)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma_{y})
for all
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
, and

(2)

g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})+g(\mathcal{H})
.

Relation (1) implies that
\sigma_{y}
satisfies hypotheses (1), (2), (3), and (4) of
\sigma_{\iota}
. Then since we have assumed that
g(\mathcal{H})\geq 10
and we must have
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 1
, we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{y}})\geq 1+10=11
, so
\BM_{\sigma_{y}}
satisfies property (4) of
\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}
.

Now, we have shown that
[\BM_{\sigma}]=[\BM_{x,y}]+[\BM_{x,z}]
. The lemma now follows by taking
s=2
and
\tau_{1}=\sigma_{y}
,
\tau_{2}=\sigma_{z}
.
∎

We now show that we can rewrite
[\BM_{\sigma}]
as a linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau}]
with
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\tau)\right|
bounded.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a,b,M\subseteq S_{g}
and
\mathcal{V}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be as in Lemma
5.2
. Let
\BM_{\sigma}\subseteq X_{g}
be a Bestvina–Margalit torus such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\leq 1
for every
1\leq i\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. Then
\BM_{\sigma}
is a linear combination of classes
[\BM_{\tau_{s}}]
for
1\leq s\leq m
such that

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\tau_{s})\right|\leq 1\text{ and }\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\tau_{s}})\leq 1

for every
1\leq i\leq j\leq 9
,
0\leq k\leq 2
and
1\leq s\leq m
.

Proof.

The proof follows by double induction on
i
and
j
. In particular, for some
(i,j)
, we assume that
\BM_{\sigma}
has the property that

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\tau_{s})\right|\leq 1

for every
0\leq k\leq 2
and for every
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)
, where
<
is the dictionary ordering. We will show that
[\BM_{\sigma}]
is a linear combination of fundamental classes
[\BM_{\tau_{1}}],\ldots,[\BM_{\tau_{m}}]
such that

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\tau_{s})\right|\leq 1

for all
0\leq k\leq 2
, for all
1\leq s\leq m
and for all
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\leq(i,j)
, and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i^{\prime},k}^{\tau_{s}})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq 9
and
0\leq k\leq 2
.

Base case:
(i,j)=(1,1)
. We have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,1,k}(\sigma)\right|=0
.

Inductive step:
(i,j)>(1,1)
. If
j=i
then we have
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)=0
for
0\leq k\leq 2
, so assume
i<j
. We will perform a double induction on the following quantities:

(1)

\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)=\max_{0\leq k\leq 2}\left\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|\right\},

(2)

\nummaxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)=\left|\left\{0\leq k\leq 2:\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|=\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)\right\}\right|
.

Reindex
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
so that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|
is maximal in the set
\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|:0\leq k\leq 2\}
. If
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|\leq 1
we are done, so assume
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|\geq 2
. Lemma
5.6
says that we can rewrite
[\BM_{\sigma}]
as a linear combination of fundamental classes
[\BM_{\sigma_{\iota}}]
, where:

•

hypothesis (1) implies that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i^{\prime},k}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq 1
for
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq 9
,

•

hypothesis (2) implies that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq 1
for all
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)
and
0\leq k\leq 2
,

•

hypothesis (3) implies that
\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)
,

•

hypothesis (3) implies that
\nummaxalg_{i,j}(\sigma_{\iota})\leq\nummaxalg_{i,j}(\sigma_{\iota})
,

•

hypothesis (4) implies that if both:

–

\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)=\maxalg_{i,j}(\sigma_{\iota})
and

–

\nummaxalg_{i,j}(\sigma)=\nummaxalg_{i,j}(\sigma_{\iota})
,

then
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|
is maximal among the set
\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma_{\iota})\right|0\leq k\leq 2\}
, and

•

hypothesis (4) implies
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma_{\iota}})\geq 11
.

Hence we may assume without loss of generality that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\sigma})\geq 10
. Choose elements
v_{i,1}^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime}_{j,1},v^{\prime\prime}_{j,1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
such that the following hold:

(1)

v_{i,1}=v^{\prime}_{i,1}+v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1}
,

(2)

v_{j,1}=v^{\prime}_{j,1}+v^{\prime\prime}_{j,1}
,

(3)

for all
(i,\ell)
and
(\ell,j)
with
(i,\ell),(\ell,j)<(i,j)
, we have

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,\ell,1}(\sigma)=\langle v^{\prime}_{i,1},v_{\ell,1}\rangle\text{ and }\theta(\mathcal{V})_{\ell,j,1}(\sigma)=\langle v_{\ell,1},v^{\prime}_{j,1}\rangle.

(4)

\left|\langle v^{\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime}_{j,1}\rangle\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|-1
,

(5)

\langle v_{i,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{j,1}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{j,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{i,1}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{i,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{i,1}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{j,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{j,1}^{\prime}\rangle=0
,

(6)

\langle v_{i,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{\ell,1}\rangle=\langle v_{j,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{\ell,1}\rangle=0
for all
\ell\neq i,j
, and

(7)

\left|\langle v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime\prime}_{j,1}\rangle\right|=1

Such elements exist since
g(\mathcal{H})\geq 11
and
\left|\mathcal{V}\right|=9
, so we can find
v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1},v_{j,1}^{\prime\prime}
with their desired algebraic intersections, and then
v^{\prime}_{i,1}=v_{i,1}-v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1}
,
v_{j,1}^{\prime}=v_{j,1}-v^{\prime\prime}_{j,1}
. Since
g(\mathcal{H}_{1})\geq 11
and
v_{i,1}
,
v_{j,1}
are primitive by hypothesis, we may choose primitive subgroups
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}
such that the following hold:

(1)

for any
x\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}
and
y\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, we have
\langle x,y\rangle=0
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1}=(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
,

(3)

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1}=\mathcal{H}_{1}
,

(4)

\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbb{Z}[a]
,

(5)

v^{\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime}_{j,1},v_{\ell,1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}
for all
\ell\neq i,j
and

(6)

v^{\prime\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime\prime}_{j,1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
. We can describe
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
using the graphical notation of Section
3
as in Figure
10
.

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\sigma}
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{1}
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\sigma}

Figure 10.
The graphical representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}

Let
\BM_{\sigma^{\prime}}
denote the Bestvina–Margalit torus corresponding to
\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
and let
\BM_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
denote the Bestvina–Margalit torus corresponding to
\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
. We will compute
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma^{\prime})
and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
as follows.

Computing
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma^{\prime})
. By our choice of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
, we have

(1)

v_{\ell,0}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=v_{\ell,0}^{\sigma}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 9
,

(2)

v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma}
for all
\ell\neq i,j
,

(3)

v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=v_{\ell,1}^{\prime}
for
\ell=1,j
,

(4)

v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma}
for all
\ell\neq i,j
, and

(5)

v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma^{\prime}}=v_{\ell,2}+v^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,1}
for
\ell=1,j
.

Now, computation (1) implies that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(\sigma^{\prime})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(\sigma)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 9
. Computations (2) and (3) and the fact that
\langle v_{i,1}^{\prime},v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma}\rangle=\langle v_{i,1},v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma}\rangle
and
\langle v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma},v_{j,1}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma},v_{j,1}\rangle
for
\ell\neq i,j
tell us that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma^{\prime})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)
for all pairs
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\neq(i,j)
. Computation (3), our assumption that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\geq 1
, and the fact that
\langle v^{\prime}_{i,1},v^{\prime}_{j,1}\rangle=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)-1
implies that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma^{\prime})\right|<\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|
. For
k=2
, computations (4) and (5) say that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2}(\sigma^{\prime})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2}(\sigma)
for all
1\leq i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\leq 9
. Finally, we see that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma^{\prime})\right|<\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\right|
if
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\right|\geq 1
. Indeed, since
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)
is positive and has

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma)\right|\geq\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\right|
, the fact that
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=0
allows us to conclude that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\leq 0
. Then
\langle v_{i,1}^{\prime\prime},v_{j,1}^{\prime\prime}\rangle=1
, so
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma^{\prime})\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)+1\right|<\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\right|
if
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,2}(\sigma)\right|\leq 1
by computation (5). Hence
\sigma^{\prime}
has either
\maxalg(\sigma^{\prime})<\maxalg(\sigma)
or
\maxalg(\sigma^{\prime})=\maxalg(\sigma)
and
\nummaxalg(\sigma^{\prime})<\nummaxalg(\sigma)
, so
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfies the inductive hypothesis for the induction on
\maxalg
and
\nummaxalg
.

Computing
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
. By our choice of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, we have

(1)

v_{\ell,0}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=v_{\ell,0}^{\sigma}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 9
,

(2)

v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=0
for all
\ell\neq i,j
,

(3)

v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=v_{\ell,1}^{\prime\prime}
for
\ell=1,j
,

(4)

v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma}+v_{\ell,1}^{\sigma}
for all
\ell\neq i,j
, and

(5)

v_{\ell,2}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=v_{\ell,2}+v^{\prime}_{\ell,1}
for
\ell=1,j
.

Then we have
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0}(\sigma)
for all
1\leq i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\leq 9
by computation (1),
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},0}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})=0
for
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\neq(i,j)
by computation (2), and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})=1
by computation (3). Then as in the previous paragraph, we see that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)
and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2}(\sigma)
have opposite signs, so for any
1\leq i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\leq 9
we have either
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\right|<\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma)\right|
or
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\right|\leq 1
. Hence
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the inductive hypothesis as well.

Since
[\BM_{\sigma}]=[\BM_{\sigma^{\prime}}]+[\BM_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]
by Lemma
3.4
, the inductive hypothesis applied to
\BM_{\sigma^{\prime}}
and
\BM_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
completes the proof.
∎

We now complete the section.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

By Lemmas
5.5
and
5.7
, any fundamental class
[\BM_{\tau}]
is a linear combination of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori
\BM_{\sigma}
with
\left|\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(v_{i,k}^{\sigma})\right|\leq 1
and
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(\sigma)\right|\leq 1
for every
1\leq i,j\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 2
. Hence by Lemma
5.2
, the vector space
H_{0}(G;\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional.
∎

6.
Finiteness of
H_{2}(\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})/\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

In this section, we will finish the proof of Proposition
1.1
. If
w\in[a]^{\perp}
, let
X_{g}^{w}
denote the full subcomplex of
X_{g}
generated by edges
e
such that the decomposition
\mathcal{H}(e)
is
compatible
with
w
, i.e.,
w\in\mathcal{H}_{i}^{e}
for some
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{e}\in\mathcal{H}(e)
. The bulk of Section
6
is devoted to proving the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
c\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating simple closed curve, and let
w=[c]
. The cokernel of the pushforward map
H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is a subquotient of
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
.

For the remainder of this section, fix a
g
,
a
, and
c
as in Lemma
6.1
. Let
X_{g}^{w,2}\subseteq X_{g}
be the subcomplex given by the union of all cells
\sigma
such that
\dim(\sigma\cap X_{g}^{w})\geq\dim(\sigma)-1
. Lemma
6.1
will proceed in two steps.

Step (1)
. We show that
\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

Step (2)
. We show that
\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q}))
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

The outline of the proof of Lemma
6.10
. Step (1) is recorded as Lemma
6.2
, and Step (2) is recorded as Lemma
6.7
. We will prove Lemma
6.2
in Section
6.1
and Lemma
6.7
on Section
6.2
. Additionally in Section
6.2
, we will assemble Lemmas
6.2
and
6.7
into the proof of Lemma
6.1
. We will conclude with Section
6.3
, where we prove Lemma
6.10
and Proposition
1.1
.

6.1.
Step (1) of the proof of Lemma
6.1

This step is recorded as the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be a nonzero primitive homology class such that the image of
w
under the adjoint map
[a]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
is nontrivial. The cokernel of the pushforward map
H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

Before proving Lemma
6.2
, we will prove a collection of auxiliary lemmas. The main goal is to prove Lemma
6.3
, which describes the cokernel of the
H_{2}
–pushforward of certain subcomplexes of
X_{g}
. Let
g,a
and
w
be as in the statement of Lemma
6.2
. If
e\subseteq X_{g}
is an edge with
w
incompatible with
\mathcal{H}(e)
let
\widehat{U}_{e}
denote the union of all 3-cells
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
such that:

•

e\subseteq\tau
,

•

there is
\mathcal{H}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(e)
such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(\tau)
,

•

there is a unique edge
e^{\prime}\subseteq\tau
with
e^{\prime}\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
, and

•

there is some
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(\tau)
with
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(e^{\prime})
.

Let
U_{e}
denote the union of all 2–cells
\sigma\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}
such that the following hold:

•

\dim(\sigma\cap e)=0
and

•

\dim(\sigma\cap X_{g}^{w})=1
.

We will prove the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
