g\geq 51
,
a\subseteq S_{g}
and
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be as in Lemma
6.1
. Let
e,f\subseteq X_{g}
be two edges such that
\mathcal{H}(e)
and
\mathcal{H}(f)
are incompatible with
w
. Furthermore, assume that
g(e)=g(f)=\{1,g-2\}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

the cokernel of the pushforward map

H_{2}(U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\widehat{U}_{e};\mathbb{Q})

is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori,

(2)

the pushforward

H_{1}(U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e};\mathbb{Q})

is an isomorphism, and

(3)

the pushforward
H_{1}(U_{e}\cap U_{f};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
is surjective.

We begin by proving the following Lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
be a 3–cell. Let
T
denote the union of all
\tau^{\prime}\subseteq X_{g}
such that
\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\mathcal{H}(\tau^{\prime})
as unordered sets. Then
T
is a 3–torus.

Proof.

This is the 3–dimensional analogue of the standard description of the 2–torus as a union of two 2–cells.
∎

If
T
is a torus as in the statement of Lemma
6.4
, we will use
\mathcal{H}(T)
to denote the decomposition
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
, except with the order forgotten. In order to prove Lemma
6.3
, we will prove the following result.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve, and
w\in[a]^{\perp}
as in Lemma
6.1
. Let
e\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge with
\mathcal{H}(e)
not compatible with
w
. Let
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}
be two 3–cells. Let
T
denote the union of all 3–cells
\tau^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}
such that
H(\tau)=H(\tau^{\prime\prime})
as unordered sets, and similarly
T^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

the pushforward
H_{1}(T\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T;\mathbb{Q})
is an isomorphism and the cokernel
H_{2}(T\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(T;\mathbb{Q})
is generated by fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori,

(2)

if
T\cap T^{\prime}
contains an edge
f
such that
f\neq e
and
f\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
, then pushforward
H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
is a surjection, and

(3)

if
T\cap T^{\prime}
does not contain an
f
as above, then the cokernel of the pushforward
H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the image of the class
[e]
.

Proof.

We prove each case in turn.

Case (1)
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\tau)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}(e)
. Since
T
is independent of the cyclic ordering on
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
by definition, we may assume without loss of generality that the decomposition of
[a]^{\perp}
given by
\{\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\perp}\}
is compatible with
w
. Given this, the intersection
T\cap U_{e}
is given by 2–cells in
X_{g}
with the following decompositions:
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
,
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\}
,
\{\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
, and
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1}\}
. This is a pair of Bestvina–Margalit tori corresponding to the unordered decompositions
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
and
\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
that intersect in the edge
\{\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\perp}\}
, so the map
H_{1}(T\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T;\mathbb{Q})
is an isomorphism and the cokernel of
H_{2}(T\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(T;\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the image of the fundamental class
[\BM_{\sigma}]
with
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
.

Case (2)
. Reuse the indexing of the elements of
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\tau^{\prime})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{3}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathcal{H}(f)=\{\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\}
. Then by the construction of
T\cap U_{e}
in the previous part, we see that
T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e}
also contains the edge corresponding to the splitting
\{\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}\}
. Therefore the class
[e]\in H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
is in the image of the pushforward
H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
, so the pushforward map is surjective.

Case (3)
. Reuse the indexing of
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
from the previous case. If
\mathcal{H}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}(\tau^{\prime})
, then
T\cap T\cap U_{e}
is the single edge corresponding to
\{\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\perp}\}
, while the intersection
T\cap T
is the Bestvina–Margalit torus containing this edge and
e
. Otherwise,
T\cap T^{\prime}=e
and so
T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e}
is the unique vertex of
X_{g}
. In either case the lemma holds.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

If
\sigma\subseteq U_{e}
is a 2–cell with
e,\sigma\subseteq\tau\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}
for some 3–cell
\tau
, then the union

\bigcup_{\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}:\mathcal{H}(\tau^{\prime})=\tau}\tau^{\prime}

is a 3–torus. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
denote the set of such 3–tori. This set is a simplicial cover of
\widehat{U}_{e}
by construction. Let

\mathcal{R}_{e}=\{T\cap U_{e}:T\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}\}.

By the definition of
U_{e}
,
\mathcal{R}_{e}
is a cover of
U_{e}
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
denote the
\Cech
–to–singular spectral sequences for
\mathcal{R}_{e}
covering
U_{e}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
covering
\widehat{U}_{e}
respectively. Then, for
k=1,2
the cokernel of
H_{k}(U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{k}(\widehat{U}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is noncanonically isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{p+q=k}\cok(\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})).

Hence to prove the first two statements in the lemma, it suffices to prove the following.

(1)

The vector space
\cok(\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q}))
is generated by fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori, and the maps
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p,q}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
are surjective for
p>0
and
p+q=2
.

(2)

The maps
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
are surjective for
p+q=1
.

We will prove each of these in turn.

The proof of statement (1)
. We first show that
\cok(\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori. Since the differentials out of
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{r}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{r}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
vanish for all
r\geq 1
, it suffices to show that
\cok(\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q}))
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori. Hence it is enough to show that for any
T\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
, the cokernel
H_{2}(T\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(T;\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori. This follows from case (1) of Lemma
6.3
.

We now show that
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is surjective for
p>0
and
p+q=2
. Consider the map of chain complexes

\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q}).

By Lemma
6.3
, the map
\mathbb{E}_{0,1}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is injective. Hence it suffices to show that the vector space
H_{1}(\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q}),\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q}))
is the zero space. Let
C_{*}
denote the quotient complex
\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})/\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
. By Lemma
6.3
, if
T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e}
contains an edge
f\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
, then the image of a class in
\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
supported on the index
T\cap T^{\prime}
is 0 in
C_{*}
. Then if no such
f
exists, Lemma
6.3
says that the cokernel
H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime}\cap U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(T\cap T^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the image of the class
[e]
. Therefore it suffices to show that if
T,T^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
, then there is a sequence
T_{0},\ldots,T_{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
such that
T=T_{0}
,
T^{\prime}=T_{n}
, and
T_{i}\cap T_{i+1}
contains an edge
f_{i}\neq e
and
f\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
. This will imply that the element in
C_{*}
given by
[e]
supported on
T\cap T^{\prime}
is homologous to the element
[e]
supported on
T_{0}\cap T_{1}+\ldots+T_{n-1}\cap T_{n}
, since the edge connecting
T
to
T^{\prime}
is homologous to the path
T_{0}\rightarrow T_{1}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow T_{n}
in the clique complex on the set
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
. Since the latter classes are all trivial in
C_{*}
, this implies that
T\cap T^{\prime}
is trivial in
H_{1}(C_{*})
as well.

Let
\mathcal{H}(e)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}\}
such that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0})=g-2
. Let
\mathcal{H}(T)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}\}
such that
\{\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{3}^{\perp}\}
is compatible with
w
. Choose
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}
such that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})=1
,
\{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\}
is incompatible with
w
, and
w^{\perp}\supseteq(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{H}(T_{1})=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}+(\mathcal{H}_{1}\cap(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp})\}
. Note that
T_{1}\cap T_{0}
contains the edge
\{\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\perp}\}
which is not
e
and is not in
X_{g}^{w}
. Now, since
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})=1
and
g\geq 51
, there is some
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(T^{\prime})
such that there is an edge
h\subseteq U_{e}
with
h\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
such that for some
\mathcal{H}_{h}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\mathcal{H}_{h}\supseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathcal{H}_{h}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}
, and
w\in\mathcal{H}_{h}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}
. Let
T_{2}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{h},\mathcal{H}_{h}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\}
and
T_{3}=\{\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{H}_{h},\mathcal{H}_{h}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{\perp}\}
. Then
T_{1}\cap T_{2}
contains the edge
\{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})^{\perp}\}
,
T_{2}\cap T_{3}
contains the edge
\{\mathcal{H}_{h},\mathcal{H}_{h}^{\perp}\}
, and
T_{3}\cap T^{\prime}
contains the edge
\{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime},(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{\perp}\}
. Hence the desired path between
T
and
T^{\prime}
exists, so
H_{1}(C_{*})=0
and thus statement (1) holds.

For
p=2
,
q=0
, note that any intersection
T_{0}\cap\ldots\cap T_{k}
of
T_{0},\ldots,T_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e}
contains the unique vertex of
X_{g}
, and in particular any such intersection is nonempty and connected. The same holds for
\mathcal{R}_{e}
, so we have
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})=0
, so the pushforward on the
r=\infty
page is surjective.

The proof of statement (2)
. As a consequence of Lemma
6.3
, the map
\mathbb{E}_{1,0}^{1}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,0}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is an isomorphism, and in particular surjective. Hence the map
\mathbb{E}_{1,0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,0}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{e};\mathbb{Q})
is surjective. The case
p=1
and
q=0
follows by the same argument as
p=2
and
q=0
, except with
2
replaced by 1.

The proof of statement (3)
. The vector space
H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by classes represented by edges, so it suffices to show that any class in
H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
represented by an edge is in the image of the pushforward
H_{1}(U_{e}\cap U_{f};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
. Now, by construction we have
U_{e}^{(1)}\cup e=\widehat{U}_{e}^{(1)}
, and similarly for
f
. Hence if
h\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f}
is an edge with
h\neq e,f
, the class
[h]
is in the image of
H_{1}(U_{e}\cap U_{f};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
. Hence it suffices to show that
[e],[f]\in H_{1}(U_{e}\cap U_{f};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f};\mathbb{Q})
if
e,f\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f}
. If
e\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}\cap\widehat{U}_{f}
, this implies that there is a 3–cell
\tau
containing both
e
and
f
. But now every edge in
\tau
besides
e
and
f
lies in both
U_{e}
and
U_{f}
by the above argument, so
[e]
is a linear combination of classes in
U_{e}\cap U_{f}
, and the lemma holds.
∎

We need one more auxiliary result before proving Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
g\geq 51
,
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve, and
w\in[a]^{\perp}
such that
w
is not sent to zero under the adjoint map
[a]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
be a 2–cell such that
\sigma\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}
. Then
\sigma
is homologous to a linear combination of 2–cells
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
such that each
\sigma_{i}\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}
, and each
\sigma_{i}
contains an edge
e_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i}
such that
\mathcal{H}(e_{i})
is not compatible with
w
and
g(e_{i})=\{1,g-2\}
.

Proof.

Inductively, it suffices to show that
\sigma
is homologous to
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3}
such that each
\sigma_{i}
contains some edge
e_{i}\subseteq\sigma_{i}
with
\min g(e_{i})<g(\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is minimal among
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
such that
\{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})^{\perp}\}
is not compatible with
w
. Choose such an
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
. Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that there are edges
e_{i}\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
with
\mathcal{H}(e_{i})=\{\mathcal{H}_{i},\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\perp}\}
. Let
\tau
be the 3–cell containing
\sigma
,
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
. Then every other 2–cell in
\partial\tau
besides
\sigma
contains either
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
. We have
\min g(e_{i})<g(\mathcal{H})
and
e_{i}\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
, so the lemma holds.
∎

We are now ready to move forward with Step (1) of the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{w}=\{e\subseteq X_{g}:\mathcal{H}(e)\text{ is incompatible with }w\text{ and }g(e)=\{1,g-2\}\}
. For each
e\in\mathcal{E}^{w}
, let
U_{e}
and
\widehat{U}_{e}
be as above. Let

\mathcal{U}=\{U_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\text{ and }\widehat{\mathcal{U}}=\{\widehat{U}_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}}.

Let
\mathcal{U}^{+}=\mathcal{U}\cup\{X_{g}^{w}\}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+}=\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\cup\{X_{g}^{w}\}
. The set
\mathcal{U}^{+}
covers
(X_{g}^{w,2})^{(2)}
, since any 2–cell
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}
is either contained in
X_{g}^{w}
, or is not. In the latter case, the cell
\sigma
must be disjoint from some edge
e\in\mathcal{E}^{w}
. Then the pushforward
H_{2}(\bigcup_{\widehat{U}\in\widehat{\mathcal{U}}}\widehat{U}\cup X_{g}^{w})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is a surjection as a consequence of Lemma
6.6
. For either of the covers
*=\mathcal{U}^{+},\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+}
, let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(*;\mathbb{Q})
denote the
\Cech
–to–singular spectral sequence corresponding to the cover
*
. The cokernel of the pushforward
H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is noncanonically identified with the direct sum

\bigoplus_{p+q=2}\cok(\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U}^{+};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+};\mathbb{Q})).

Therefore, it suffices to prove the following three facts:

(1)

The cokernel of the map
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\mathcal{U};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}},\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori,

(2)

the cokernel of the map
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathcal{U};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is trivial, and

(3)

the cokernel of the map
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{3}(\mathcal{U};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{3}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is trivial.

We prove each of these in turn.

Proof of Fact (1)
. This is the content of statement (1) of Lemma
6.3
.

Proof of Fact (2)
. By statements (2) and (3) respectively of Lemma
6.3
, the maps
\mathbb{E}_{0,1}^{1}(\mathcal{U}^{+};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+};\mathbb{Q})
and
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}(\mathcal{U}^{+};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+};\mathbb{Q})
are an isomorphism and a surjection respectively. Hence the map
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathcal{U}^{+};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+};\mathbb{Q})
is surjective.

Proof of Fact (3)
. For any choice of
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{w}
, both
U_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{e_{k}}
and
\widehat{U}_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap\widehat{U}_{e_{k}}
contain the unique vertex of
X_{g}
, and hence are connected. The same applies if we include
X_{g}^{w}
, so
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\mathcal{U}^{+};\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}^{+};\mathbb{Q})=0
.

Completing the proof
. Given the above three statements,
\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is generated by a quotient of the image
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
, and the image of this map is generated by the images of the fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori, so the lemma holds.
∎

6.2.
Step (2) of the proof of Lemma
6.1

We now prove Lemma
6.7
. This will complete the proof of Lemma
6.1
. We will also prove Lemma
6.8
, which is an auxiliary result about the acyclicity of a complex where the vertices are edges
e\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be a nonzero primitive homology class such that the image of
w
under the adjoint map
\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
is nontrivial. The vector space

\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q}))

is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori.

We begin by defining an auxiliary complex. Let
e\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge with
g(e)=\{1,g-2\}
. Let
A_{e}\subseteq H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
be the affine space given by

A_{e}=\left\{[f]\in H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}):[f]-[e]\in H_{1}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\right\}.

Let
Y(e)
denote the connected component containing the edge
e
of the complex
C(e)
, where a
k
–cell of
C(e)
is a set of
ordered

(k+1)
edges
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}\subseteq X_{g}
such that:

•

g(e_{i})=\{1,g-2\}
for every
0\leq i\leq k
,

•

[e_{i}]\in A_{e}
for every
0\leq i\leq k
, and

•

there is an edge
e^{\prime}
such that
[e^{\prime}]\in A_{e}
and such that
e^{\prime}
shares a 2–cell
\sigma_{i}\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}
with each
e_{i}
.

Remark
. Note that the cells of
Y(e)
are ordered collections of vertices. This is to avoid certain technical complications later in the section.

We will prove the following auxiliary result.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be a primitive nonzer class such that
w
is not sent to zero under the adjoint map
[a]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
. Let
e\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge with
e\not\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
and
g(e)=\{1,g-2\}
. The complex
Y(e)
is 1–acylic.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}(e)=\{V_{0},V_{1}\}
. Let
w_{0},w_{1}
be two nonzero elements in
[a]^{\perp}
such that
w_{i}\in V_{i}
and
w_{0}+w_{1}=w
. We will prove the following.

Claim
. Let
f\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge with
f
a vertex of
Y(e)
and let
\mathcal{H}(f)=\left\{V_{0}^{f},V_{1}^{f}\right\}
. Then after possibly reindexing, we have
w_{i}\in V_{i}^{f}
for
i=0,1
.

Proof of claim
. Note that for any 2–cell
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
such that
w_{0},w_{1}
compatible with
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
, there is some
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
with
w_{0},w_{1}\in\mathcal{H}
or
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
, so such a
\sigma
is contained in
X_{g}^{w,2}
. Likewise, if
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}^{w,2}
is a 2–cell containing
e
, then there is
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
compatible with
w
, so in particular we have
w_{0},w_{1}
both compatible with
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
. Hence if
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}
is a cell in
Y(e)
with
e^{\prime}
as in the definition of
Y(e)
, then
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}
and
e^{\prime}
are all compatible with
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
, so the claim holds.

Now, given the claim, we see that since
g\geq 51
, any triple of edges
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\in Y(e)
are the vertices of a 2–cell in
Y(e)
. Indeed, for any three
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\subseteq X_{g}
with
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\in Y(e)
, we have
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
compatible with
\mathcal{H}(e_{0})
,
\mathcal{H}(e_{1})
and
\mathcal{H}(e_{2})
by the claim. Since
g\geq 51
and
g(e_{k})=(1,g-2)
for
k=0,1,2
, we see that there is some primitive
\mathcal{H}\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
with
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{Z}[a]
,
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}+\mathcal{H}=[a]^{\perp}
, where
w_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
,
w_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
, and
\mathcal{H}
compatible with
\mathcal{H}(e_{k})
for
k=0,1,2
. Let
e^{\prime}\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(e^{\prime})
. For each
e_{k}
, let
\sigma_{k}
be a 2–cell containing
e_{k}
and
e^{\prime}
. We see that since
w_{0},w_{1}
both compatible with
e_{k}
and
e^{\prime}
, then the third edge
z_{k}
of
\sigma_{k}
must have
w_{0},w_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{k}}
or
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z_{k}}
. Hence
\sigma_{k}\in X_{g}^{w,2}
, so
[e^{\prime}]\in A_{e}
since
[e^{\prime}]+[z_{k}]=[e_{k}]
and
[z_{k}]\in H_{1}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore by the definition of
Y(e)
, we have that
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}
is a 2–cell. This implies that
Y(e)
is the 2–skeleton of a flag complex on the complete graph of the vertices of
Y(e)
, so in particular we have
H_{1}(Y(e);\mathbb{Q})=0
.
∎

We are now ready to complete Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{w}_{1}=\{e\subseteq X_{g}:e\text{ is incompatible with }w,g(e)=\{1,g-2\}\}
. Let
U_{e}\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
be the subcomplex generated by all
2
–cells
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}^{w}
such that
e
and
\sigma
are both faces of a
3
–cell
\tau
. Let
\widehat{U}_{e}
consist of the union of all
3
cells
\tau
as in the previous sentence. For any such
\tau\subseteq\widehat{U}_{e}
, the union of
\tau
with all
\tau_{i}
that
H(\tau_{i})=H(\tau)
as unordered sets forms a
k+1
–torus, and this torus is naturally isomorphic to the product of
e
and the minimal
k
–torus in
X_{g}
containing
\sigma
. Hence, there is a natural isomorphism
\widehat{U}_{e}\cong e\times U_{e}
. By construction, the collection

\overline{\mathcal{U}}=\{\widehat{U}_{e}/U_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{w}}

covers the 2–skeleton of
X_{g}^{w,2}/X_{w}
. For any cover
*=\mathcal{U},\widehat{\mathcal{U}}
, or
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, let

\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(*;\mathbb{Q})
denote the
\Cech
–to–singular spectral sequence corresponding to the cover
*
. Since
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
covers the 2–skeleton of
X_{g}^{w,2}/X_{g}^{w}
, we have

\bigoplus_{p+q=2}\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})\twoheadrightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2}/X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q}).

From the long exact sequence in homology for the pair
(X_{g}^{w,2},X_{g}^{w})
, we have an inclusion

\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q}))\hookrightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2},X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q}).

Then we have
H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2},X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})=H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2}/X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})=0
for
p<0
or
q<0
, the vector space
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is a quotient of
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore it is enough to prove the following three facts:

(1)

The image of
\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0,2}^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{U}},\mathbb{Q})
is generated by Bestvina–Margalit tori,

(2)

the vector space
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is the
0
–space, and

(3)

the vector space
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is the
0
–space.

We prove each of these in turn.

The proof of Fact (1)
. Since
\widehat{U}_{e}\cong e\times U_{e}
, the cokernel
\cok(H_{2}(U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(\widehat{U}_{e};\mathbb{Q}))
is isomorphic to
H_{1}(U_{e};\mathbb{Q})\otimes H_{1}(e;\mathbb{Q})
by the Künneth formula. The tensor product of a class represented by an edge
f\subseteq U_{e}
with
e
is the Bestvina–Margalit torus containing
e
and
f
, so Fact (1) holds.

The proof of Fact (2)
. Let
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{w}
. By construction, we have

\dim\left(H_{1}\left(\widehat{U}_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap\widehat{U}_{e_{k}}/U_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{e_{k}};\mathbb{Q}\right)\right)\leq 1

so
\mathbb{E}_{*,1}^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})
is the cellular chain complex of a simplicial complex
Z
, where the
k
–cells of
Z
are sets of
k+1
edges
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{w}
with

\dim\left(H_{1}\left(\widehat{U}_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap\widehat{U}_{e_{k}}/U_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{e_{k}};\mathbb{Q}\right)\right)=1.

Now, note that if
U_{e_{0}},\ldots,U_{e_{k}}
form a
k
–cell in
Z
, then there is an edge
f\subseteq X_{g}
with
[f]\not\in H_{1}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})
such that
f
and
e_{i}
are two edges of a 2–cell
\sigma_{i}\subseteq X_{g}
with the third edge in
X_{g}^{w}
. Hence if
e\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{w}
, the path component
P_{e}
of
Z
containing
\widehat{U}_{e}/U_{e}
has 2–skeleton canonically identified with the 2–skeleton of
Y(e)
. Therefore
H_{1}(P_{e};\mathbb{Q})=0
by Lemma
6.8
. Therefore
H_{1}(Z;\mathbb{Q})=0
, so
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})=0
as desired.

The proof of Fact (3)
. For any choice of
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{w}
, both
U_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{e_{k}}
and
\widehat{U}_{e_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap\widehat{U}_{e_{k}}
contain the unique vertex of
X_{g}
, and hence are connected. Therefore
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{U}};\mathbb{Q})=0
.
∎

We now prove Lemma
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

There is a noncanonical surjection

\displaystyle\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q}))\bigoplus\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w,2};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{v};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

Hence the lemma follows by Lemmas
6.2
and
6.7
.
∎

6.3.
The proof of Proposition
1.1

We now conclude Section
6
. We first connect the results of Section
4
with Lemma
6.1
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be a nonzero primitive element such that
w
is not sent to zero under the adjoint map
[a]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\varphi
be the composition

H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))/H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})

where the first map is the first map is the map in the five term exact sequence for the equivariant homology spectral sequence given by the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Then the map
\varphi
is surjective.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1
, the quotient space
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})
is generated by the images of fundamental classes of Bestvina–Margalit tori. Hence it suffices to show that any
[\BM_{\sigma}]\in H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the image of some
[T_{c,c^{\prime}},T_{d,d^{\prime}}]\in H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
under the map
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\BM_{\sigma}
be a Bestvina–Margalit torus, and let
\widehat{\sigma}
be a lift of
\sigma
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
such that
a\in\widehat{\sigma}
. Let
a_{1},a_{2}
be the other two vertices of
\widehat{\sigma}
. Choose a curve
b\subseteq S_{g}
such that the geometric intersections
\left|a\cap b\right|=\left|a_{1}\cap b\right|=\left|a_{2}\cap b\right|
are all equal to 1. Now, there are curves
b_{1},b_{2}
such that
b\cup b_{1}
and
b\cup b_{2}
are bounding pairs, and the corresponding bounding pair maps
T_{b,b_{1}}
and
T_{b,b_{2}}
both commute and take
a
to
a_{1}
and
a
to
a_{2}
respectively. The construction of such bounding pairs can be seen in Figure
11
.

b
b_{1}
b_{2}
a_{2}
a
a_{1}

Figure 11.
The curves
a,a_{1},a_{2}
and bounding pairs
b\cup b_{1}
and
b\cup b_{2}

Let
\gamma_{i}
denote the loop in
\pi_{1}(X_{g})
given by the image of the edge in
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
connecting
a
to
a_{i}
. The bounding pair map
T_{b,b_{i}}
is sent to the loop
\gamma_{i}
under the natural map
\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{g})
for
i=1,2
. Hence the image of the abelian cycle
[T_{b,b_{1}},T_{b,b_{2}}]
under the map
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the abelian cycle
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\in H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, which is
[\BM_{\sigma}]
.
∎

We now prove Lemma
6.10
, which verifies that the first hypothesis of Proposition
2.1
holds for
\Sp([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
,
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, and
d=1
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
g\geq 51
and let
w\in[a]^{\perp}
be a primitive element such that the image of
w
under the adjoint map
[a]^{\perp}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}([a]^{\perp},\mathbb{Z})
is nonzero. Then the vector space
\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional.

Proof of
6.10
.

Let
d
be a representative of
w
disjoint from
a
, and let
G=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup d))\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. We will show that the
G
–representation
V=\cok(H_{2}(X_{g}^{w};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
2.1
for
\delta=9
, namely:

(1)

for
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup d)
a nonseparating multicurve with
|M|\geq 9
, the map

\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V

is surjective, and

(2)

for
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup d)
a nonseparating multicurve with
|M|\leq 8
, and
G_{M}=\Stab_{G}(M)
, the coinvariants module

H_{0}(G_{M};V)

is finite dimensional.

Then
V
is finite dimensional by Proposition
2.1
.

Hypothesis (1)
. Lemma
6.9
tells us that the map

H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow V

is surjective. Now Proposition
4.1
tells us that, if
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a\cup d)
is a nonseparating multicurve with
|M|\geq 9
, the map

\bigoplus_{c\in M}H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T}_{c}\rightarrow H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

is surjective. Then the image
H_{2}^{\ab,\bp}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V
is contained in
V^{T_{c}}
, so the map

\bigoplus_{c\in M}V^{T_{c}}\rightarrow V

is surjective, as desired.

Hypothesis (2)
. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}(a{\ssearrow}d)
be a multicurve with
|M|\leq 8
. The coinvariants module
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G_{M}}
is finite dimensional by Lemma
5.1
applied to
M^{\prime}=M\cup d
. Now by Lemma
6.1
, there is a surjection
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow V
, so there is a surjection
\BM_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G_{M}}\rightarrow V_{G_{M}}
, so in particular
V_{G_{M}}
is surjective.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.1
.

Let
G=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}a)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. We will use Proposition
2.1
applied to the
G
–representation
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
with
d=1
to show that
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional. In particular, we will show that:

(1)

for any nonseparating
c\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
, we have
\cok(H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})^{T_{c}}\rightarrow H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
finite dimensional, and

(2)

the coinvariants module
H_{2}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G}
is finite dimensional.

Hypothesis (1)
. This is exactly the content of Lemma
6.10
.

Hypothesis (2)
. Let
\mathcal{I}(\vec{x})\subseteq\Mod(S_{g})
denote the partial Torelli group defined by Putman
[
26
]
, which is the subgroup of
\Mod(S_{g})
acting trivially on the homology class
\vec{x}
. Now,
\mathcal{I}(\vec{x})
fits into a short exact sequence

1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(\vec{x})\rightarrow G\rightarrow 1.

For
g\geq 3
, we know that
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional by the work of Johnson
[
13
]
. Therefore by the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre
[
5
]
spectral sequence and the fact that
G
is finitely presented
[
27
]
, we see that
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})_{G}
is finite dimensional if and only if
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}(\vec{x});\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional. We now consider the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}(\vec{x})
on
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}(S_{g})
. This action is cocompact on
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}(S_{g})
. Furthermore, the stabilizers of vertices and edges are finitely presented and finitely generated respectively, since these are just mapping class groups of surfaces which are finitely presented
[
8
]
. Then
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}(S_{g})
is
(g-3)
–acyclic by a theorem of the author
[
21
]
, so
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}(\vec{x});\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional, and thus the second hypothesis is satisfied.
∎

7.
Finiteness of coinvariants in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g},C_{[a]}(S_{g}))

The main work of this section is to prove the following result.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
G=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Then the vector space

H_{0}(G;\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2})

is finite dimensional.

Notation
. For the remainder of this section, we will fix
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating simple closed curve. We will also fix
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
as in the statement of Lemma
7.1
. We will use
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
to denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence given by the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Additionally, for the remainder of the section, we will let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
.

The outline of the proof of Lemma
7.1
. The strategy used is similar to that used in the proof of Lemma
5.1
. In particular, we will associate to each edge
e\subseteq X_{g}
certain invariants that record how the elements of
\mathcal{V}
project onto the elements of
H(e)
. We will show in Lemma
7.2
that these numbers are preserved under the action of
G
. We then prove Lemma
7.4
, which describes an intermediate quotient between
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
We then describe in Lemmas
7.5
and
7.7
how these invariants change under addition of edges. With these results in hand, we will prove Lemma
7.1
.

Algebraic invariants of edges
. Let
[b]\in H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
be a nonzero primitive class such that
\langle[a],[b]\rangle=1
and
[b]
intersects any element of
\mathcal{V}
trivially. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge and let
\mathcal{H}(x)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\}
. For each
1\leq i\leq 8
,
0\leq k\leq 1
, let
v_{i,k}^{x}=\proj_{[a]^{\perp}\cap[b]^{\perp}}v_{i}
. We define the following two invariants of the edge
x
:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)
is the maximal
n
such that
v^{x}_{i,k}=nv
for some
v\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{x}
and

(2)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)=\langle v^{x}_{i,k},v^{x}_{j,k}\rangle
.

We have the following result about these algebraic invariants, which parallels Lemma
5.2
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve such that
b
has geometric intersection number with
a
, and such that
b
intersects
M
trivially. Let
G=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}M)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. After possibly relabeling
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}
, suppose the following hold:

(1)

g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})=g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y})
,

(2)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y)
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
and

(3)

\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)=\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(y)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
.

Then there is a
g\in G
such that
gx=y
.

Proof.

This follows by a similar argument to Lemma
5.2
, except with 2–cells replaced by edges. The
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}
here is the same as the
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}
in Lemma
5.2
, and similarly for
\theta(\mathcal{V})
and
\theta(\mathcal{V})
.
∎

Before proving Lemmas
7.5
and
7.7
, we will prove Lemma
7.4
, which describes an intermediate quotient between
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
. We begin by showing that
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is a quotient of
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. The inclusion map
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}/d_{2,1}^{1}(\mathbb{E}_{2,1}^{1})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The lemma is equivalent to the statement that the differential
d_{1,1}^{1}
is the zero map. Since
g\geq 51\geq 4
, a theorem of the author
[
21
, Theorem A]
says that we have
H_{1}(C_{[a]}(S_{g});\mathbb{Z})=0
. Hence

\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}\Rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\text{ for }p+q=1

by the properties of the equivariant homology spectral sequence
[
5
, Section VII]
. Therefore there is an exact sequence

\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1,1}^{1}}\mathbb{E}_{0,1}^{1}\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}).

Since
\mathbb{E}_{0,1}^{1}\cong H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})
, the exact sequence is

\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1,1}^{1}}H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}).

A theorem of Putman
[
25
, Theorem B]
says that the map
H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is an injection, so
d_{1,1}^{1}=0
.
∎

As in Section
4
, let
\tau_{g}:\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
denote the Johnson homomorphism
[
11
]
. If
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
is a cell, let
A_{\sigma}
denote the vector space

\im\left(\bigoplus_{\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}.

We have the following result about
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. The surjection
\varphi:\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
induced by quotienting out the subspace
d_{2,1}^{1}(\mathbb{E}_{2,1}^{1})
factors through the natural projection

\rho:\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}/d_{2,1}^{1}(\mathbb{E}_{2,1}^{1})=\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
denote the quotient map, which exists by Lemma
7.3
. For each
x\subseteq X_{g}
, let
\widehat{x}\subseteq C_{[a]}(S_{g})
be a lift of
x
such that
a
is a vertex of
\widehat{x}
. Let

A_{\widehat{x}}=\im\left(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right).

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{x}
. Lemma
4.5
implies that
\im(H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is sent to
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\im(\tau_{g})\otimes\mathbb{Q})
under the Johnson homomorphism, and similarly for
S^{\prime}
. Therefore we have
A_{\widehat{x}}=A_{x}
by the definition of
A_{x}
. Hence it suffices to show that
\varphi
factors through the quotient map

\rho:\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}.

Showing that
\varphi
factors through
\rho
is equivalent to showing that
\ker(\rho)\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
, so it suffices to show that

\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}\ker\left(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right)\subseteq\ker(\varphi).

The idea of the proof is to rewrite any class in
\ker(\rho)
as a linear combination of classes, where the edges in these classes have sufficiently large genus. If
x\subseteq X_{g}
is an edge and
f\in H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})
is a class, we use the notation
\left(x,f\right)\in\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})
to denote the class in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
equal to
f
in the index
x
and equal to zero in every other index. Now, we have

\ker(\rho)=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}\ker\left(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\right),

and the latter space is spanned by elements of the form
(x,f)
. Hence it suffices to show that
(x,f)\in\ker(\varphi)
for any
x\in X_{g}^{(1)}
and
f\in\ker(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Let
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}
be the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{x}
. We have a surjection
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\oplus H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q}
by applying the Künneth formula to the product
\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})\times\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime\prime},S_{g})
, so we may assume that
f
is supported on one connected component
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{x}
. Without loss of generality, we will assume that
f\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
. If
g(S^{\prime})\geq 3
, then Lemma
4.5
says that the map
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is injective. Since
f
is in the kernel of the map
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, this implies that
f=0
, so
(x,f)=0\in\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{1}
and thus
(x,f)\in\ker(\varphi)
. Otherwise,
g(S^{\prime})\leq 2
, which implies in particular that
g(S^{\prime\prime})\geq 4
. Let
\sigma\subseteq C_{[a]}(S_{g})
be a 2–cell such that:

•

\widehat{x}\subseteq\sigma

•

for the other two edges
y,z
of
\sigma
, the connected components
S_{y}^{\prime},S_{z}^{\prime}
of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}y
and
S_{g}{\ssearrow}z
respectively that contain
S^{\prime}
have
g(S_{y}^{\prime}),g(S_{z}^{\prime})\geq 3
.

Now, let
\overline{\sigma}
denote the image of
\sigma
in
X_{g}
and similarly for
\overline{y}
and
\overline{z}
. We have chosen
\sigma
so that
f
has a representative
F\in\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\sigma)
. Therefore, after possibly reorienting
\sigma
,
x
and
y
, we have a relation in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by
d_{2,1}^{1}(\overline{\sigma},[F])
, which tells us in particular that

0=(\overline{y},[F])+(\overline{z},[F])-(x,f)

in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
. We have
F
supported on
S_{y}^{\prime}
and
S_{z}^{\prime}
, both of which have genus at least 3. Then by Lemma
4.5
, the map
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S_{y}^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is injective, and similarly for
S_{z}^{\prime}
. But now, we have assumed that
f\in\ker(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Therefore
[F]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S_{y}^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
and
[F]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S_{z}^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
are both zero, so
(\overline{y},[F])=(\overline{z},[F])=0
. Since
d_{2,1}^{1}(\overline{\sigma},[F])\in\ker(\varphi)
by the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
, we have
(x,f)\in\ker(\varphi)
, as desired.
∎

Notation
. For the remainder of the paper, if
x\subseteq X_{g}
is an edge and
f\in A_{x}
is a class, we will denote the corresponding element in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
under the image of the quotient map
\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
by
(x,f)
.

7.1.
The proof of Lemma
7.6

We now continue with the definitions. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge. Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
be another edge such that
x,y
are two edges of a 2–cell
\sigma
, and let
z
denote the third edge of
\sigma
. We say that such a
y
is
rank
\mathcal{V}
–shrinking relative to
x
if, after possibly relabeling
\mathcal{H}(x)
,
\mathcal{H}(y)
and
\mathcal{H}(z)
, the following hold:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)\geq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y)
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z)
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
,
0\leq k\leq 1
, and

(2)

for at least one choice of
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
with

\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)\geq\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{j,k}(x),:1\leq j\leq 8,0\leq k\leq 1\}

we have
\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)\}>\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z).

If
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
, we say that
y
is
rank
\mathcal{V}
–shrinking relative to
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
if, in addition, there is
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{y}\in\mathcal{H}(y)
with
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{y}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
. Denote the set of rank
\mathcal{V}
–shrinking edges relative to
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
by
\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
. We now prove Lemma
7.5
, which will allow us to rewrite classes in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
as linear combinations of classes with lower
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve such that
b
has geometric intersection number with
a
, and such that
b
intersects
M
trivially. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge. Suppose that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 12
, and that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)>1
for at least one
1\leq i\leq 8
.
Then the natural map

\varphi:\bigoplus_{y\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})}A_{y}\cap A_{x}\rightarrow A_{x}

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{x}
. We will show that
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{y}
for some
y\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}).
If
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
then
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\subseteq\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}\subseteq A_{y}
for any
y\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
, so it remains to prove the result in the case that
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}.
Suppose without loss of generality that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)>1
and

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)=\max\{\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)\}\geq\max\{\max\{\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{j,0}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{j,1}(x)\}:1\leq j\leq 8\}.

For each
1\leq i\leq 8
, let
w_{i,k}
denote the nonzero primitive homology class in
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
such that

\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{x}\cap[b]^{\perp}}(v_{i})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)w_{i,k}.

We claim that there is a nonzero primitive class
u\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
such that:

•

\langle u,w_{i,0}\rangle=0
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
,

•

\langle u,r_{s}\rangle=0
for all
1\leq s\leq 3
, and

•

u
is not in the span of
\{w_{1,0},\ldots,w_{8,1},r_{1},\ldots,r_{3}\}
.

Such a
u
exists since we have assumed that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 12
and there are only eleven elements in the set
\{w_{1,0},\ldots,w_{8,0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\}
, so the subspace
w_{1,0}^{\perp}\cap\ldots w_{8,0}^{\perp}\cap r_{1}^{\perp}\cap r_{2}^{\perp}\cap r_{3}^{\perp}
has genus at least one. Let
h=w_{1,0}-u.
Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge such that such that:

•

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}

•

u\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}
,

•

h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
,

•

w_{i,0}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}
for all
2\leq i\leq 8
, and

•

r_{s}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
for all
1\leq s\leq 3.

Let
\sigma
be a 2–cell containing
x
and
y
, and let
z
be the third edge of
\sigma
. Index
\mathcal{H}(z)
so that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
. By construction, the vectors
\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k}^{y}}(v_{i})
and
\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k}^{z}}(v_{i})
are given as follows:

(1)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}}(v_{1})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x)u
,

(2)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}}(v_{i})=0
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(3)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}}(v_{1})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)w_{1,1}+\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}h
,

(4)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}}(v_{i})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)w_{i,1}+\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x)w_{i,0}
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(5)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}}(v_{1})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x)h,

(6)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}}(v_{i})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x)w_{i,0}
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(7)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}}(v_{1})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)w_{1,1}+\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}u
, and

(8)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}}(v_{i})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}w_{i,1}
for
2\leq i\leq 8
.

By assumption, the homology classes
w_{i,k}
,
h
and
u
are all primitive. Hence the numbers
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y)
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z)
are given as follows, where each relation in the following list follows from the corresponding relation in the previous list:

(a)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(y)=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x)
,

(b)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(y)=0
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(c)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(y)=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x))
,

(d)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(y)=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x))
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(e)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(z)=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x)
,

(f)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(z)=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x)
for
2\leq i\leq 8
,

(g)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(z)=\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x))
,

(h)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(z)=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,1}(x)
for
2\leq i\leq 8.

We have assumed that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)>1
. Since
v_{1}
is primitive, we have
\gcd(\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x))=1
, so relation (c) implies that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(y)<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x)
. Similarly, relation (g) implies that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(z)<\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,1}(x).
Then relations (a), (b) and (d) imply that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y)\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
, and relations (e), (f) and (h) imply that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z)\leq\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y)
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
, and thus
y\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
. Then we have chosen
y
so that
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}
, so
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{y}
, and thus the proof is complete.
∎

We use Lemma
7.5
to prove the following, which is the first step of the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve such that
b
has geometric intersection number with
a
, and such that
b
intersects
M
trivially. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Let
(x,f)\in\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
be a class. There is a relation in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by

(x,f)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\lambda_{\ell}(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})

such that
\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y_{\ell})\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 8,0\leq k\leq 1
, and
1\leq\ell\leq m
.

Proof.

Let
\maxrk(x)=\max_{1\leq i\leq 8,0\leq k\leq 1}\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)
. Let
\nummaxrk(x)
be the number of pairs
(i,k)
with
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)=\maxrk(x)
. The proof proceeds by double induction on
\maxrk(x)
and
\nummaxrk(x)
.

Base case:
\maxrk(x)=1
. In this, the resulting linear relation is
(x,f)=(x,f)
.

Inductive step: the lemma holds for all
y\subseteq X_{g}
that satisfy either
\maxrk(y)<\maxrk(x)
or both
\maxrk(y)\leq\maxrk(x)
and
\nummaxrk(y)<\nummaxrk(x)
. We want to apply Lemma
7.5
. We begin with the the following claim.

Claim
. There is a linear relation

(x,f)=\sum_{p=1}^{q}(z_{p},f_{p})

such that for every
1\leq p\leq q
:

•

either
\maxrk(z_{p})=\maxrk(x)
and
\nummaxrk(z_{p})\leq\nummaxrk(x)
, or
\maxrk(z_{p})<\maxrk(x)
, and

•

if
\maxrk(z_{p})\neq 1
, there is a pair
(i,k)
such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z_{p})=\maxrk(z_{p})
and
g(\mathcal{H}_{k}^{z_{p}})\geq 13
.

Proof of claim
. By reindexing, we may assume without loss of generality that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{1,0}(x)=\maxrk(x)
. If
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 13
then we are done, so suppose that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\leq 12
. Since
f\in A_{x}
, we may rewrite
f
as a
\mathbb{Q}
–linear combination of pure tensors
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{x}
such that each
r_{i}
is primitive. Therefore we may assume without loss of generality that
f=r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}
with
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{x}
for some
0\leq k\leq 1
. Now, since
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\leq 12
, we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})\geq 51-12\geq 13
. Hence there is a primitive subgroup
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
such that the following hold:

•

2*g(\mathcal{H})+1=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H})
,

•

g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})-g(\mathcal{H})\leq 11
,

•

[a]\in\mathcal{H}
,

•

r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},v_{1,1},v_{2,1},\ldots,v_{8,1}\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
.

Let
z_{1}\subseteq X_{g}
be the unique edge with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(z_{1})
. By construction,
z_{1}
and
x
share a 2–cell
\sigma\subseteq X_{g}
that satisfies
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\}
. Let
z_{2}
be the third edge of
\sigma
. We have
\mathcal{H}(z_{1})=\{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\}
and
\mathcal{H}(z_{2})=\{\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}+\mathcal{H}\}
. Since
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}
are all in either
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
or
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
and are in
\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
by hypothesis, the fact that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\}
implies that
f\in A_{\sigma}
. Therefore in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
, the image of
d_{2,1}^{1}(\sigma,f)
(after possibly reorienting
x,z_{1}
and
z_{2}
) yields a relation

(x,f)=(z_{1},f)+(z_{2},f).

Assume that
\mathcal{H}(z_{1})
is indexed so that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{1}}
. By our choice of
\mathcal{H}
, the projections of each
v_{i}
to the elements of
\mathcal{H}(z_{1})
are given as follows:

•

v_{i,1}^{z_{1}}=v_{i}
, and

•

v_{i,0}^{z_{1}}=0
.

Since each
v_{i}
is primitive by assumption, we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z_{1})=1
or
0
for every
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
. Hence
(z_{1},f)
satisfies the desired properties of
z_{i}
in the claim, since:

•

\maxrk(z_{1})=1<\maxrk(x)
, and

•

z_{1}
does not satisfy the hypothesis in the second condition.

For the class
(z_{2},f)
, after indexing
\mathcal{H}(z_{2})
so that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{2}}
, the projections
v_{i,k}^{z_{2}}
satisfy
v_{i,k}^{z_{2}}=v_{i,k}^{x}
. Hence we have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z_{2})=\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)
for
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
. Now, by construction we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{2}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})+g(\mathcal{H})
. Since we have chosen
\mathcal{H}
so that
g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})-g(\mathcal{H})\leq 11
, we have
g(\mathcal{H})\geq g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})-11
. Then we have

g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{2}})=g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})+g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})\geq g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})+g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})-11\geq 51-1-11\geq 13,

since we have assumed that
g=g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})+g(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x})+1\geq 51
. Therefore
z_{2}
satisfies the properties in the claim, since:

•

\maxrk(z_{2})=\maxrk(x)
and
\nummaxrk(z_{2})=\nummaxrk(x)
, and

•

any pair
(i,0)
that satisfies
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x)=\maxrk(x)
also satisfies
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(z_{2})=\maxrk(z_{2})
, and
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z_{2}})\geq 13
,

so the claim holds.

We now continue with the inductive step of the proof. By the claim, we may rewrite the class
(x,f)
as a sum so that each summand
(z,f^{\prime})
satisfies
\maxrk(z)\leq\maxrk(x)
, and
\nummaxrk(z)\leq\nummaxrk(x)
if
\maxrk(z)=\maxrk(x)
, and, if
(z,f)
does not already satisfy
\maxrk(z)\leq 1
, we have
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z})\geq 13
with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(z)=\maxrk(z)
for some
i
. Hence we may assume without loss of generality that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 13
and
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,0}(x)=\maxrk(x)
for some
1\leq i\leq 8
. Then by Lemma
7.5
, there is a collection of edges
y_{\ell}\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
and a choice of
f_{\ell}\in A_{y_{\ell}}\cap A_{x}
such that

f=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}.

For each
y_{\ell}
, there is a 2–cell
\sigma_{\ell}\subseteq X_{g}
with
x_{\ell},y_{\ell}\in X_{g}
by the definition of
\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
. For each of these
\sigma_{\ell}
, let
z_{\ell}\subseteq\sigma_{\ell}
denote the third edge besides
x
and
y_{\ell}
. Then
f_{\ell}\in A_{y_{\ell}}\cap A_{x}
and
A_{x}\cap A_{y_{\ell}}=A_{\sigma_{\ell}}
by the definition of
A_{\tau}
for any
\tau\subseteq X_{g}
, so we have
f_{\ell}\in A_{\tau}
. Therefore we have the following relation in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
:

d_{2,1}^{1}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(\sigma_{\ell},f_{\ell})\right)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(x,f_{\ell})+(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})-(z_{\ell},f_{\ell}).

But now, by rearranging terms, we have a relation

\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(x,f_{\ell})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})-(z_{\ell},f_{\ell}).

Then since
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}=f
, we have a relation

(x,f)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(z_{\ell},f_{\ell})-(y_{\ell},f_{\ell}).

Since
y_{\ell}\in\rkshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})
, for any
1\leq\ell\leq m
, we have either
\maxrk(y_{\ell})<\maxrk(x)
, or
\maxrk(y_{\ell})=\maxrk(x)
and
\nummaxrk(y_{\ell})<\nummaxrk(x)
, and similarly for
z_{\ell}
. Therefore by the inductive hypothesis,
y_{\ell}
and
z_{\ell}
are linear combinations of elements as in the statement of the lemma, so the lemma holds for
(x,f)
as well.
∎

7.2.
The proof of Lemma
7.8

We now proceed to the second step of the proof of Lemma
7.1
. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1.
Let
y\subseteq X_{g}
be another edge such that
y
and
x
are two edges of a 2–cell
\sigma
, and let
z
be the third edge of
\sigma.
We say that
y
is
algebraically
\mathcal{V}
–shrinking relative to
x
if, after possibly reindexing
\mathcal{H}(x)
,
\mathcal{H}(y)
, and
\mathcal{H}(z)
, the following hold:

(1)

\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z)\leq 1
for
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
,

(2)

\max\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(x)\right|,1\}\geq\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(y)\right|,\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(z)\right|
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 8
, and

(3)

at least one pair
1\leq i<j\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
with

\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\right|=\max\{\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k}(x)\right|:1\leq i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\leq 8,0\leq k\leq 1\}

has
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\right|>\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(y)\right|,\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(z)\right|
.

If
x
is an edge, let
\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)\subseteq X_{g}^{(1)}
denote the set of algebraically
\mathcal{V}
–shrinking relative to
x
edges in
X_{g}
. We will prove the following result about
\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
, which completes the second step of the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve such that
b
has geometric intersection number with
a
, and such that
b
intersects
M
trivially. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
. Suppose that there is a pair
1\leq i<j\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
such that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\right|>1
. Then the natural map

\varphi:\bigoplus_{y\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)}A_{y}\cap A_{x}\rightarrow A_{x}

is surjective.

Proof.

Choose three primitive classes
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cup\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
such that
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{x}
. We will show that
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in\im(\varphi)
. Since
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(x)\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(x)\right|
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 8
because we have assumed that
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=0
, we may assume without loss of generality that
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq\frac{51}{2}
, so in particular
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 13
. If
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
then we are done since
\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
is nonempty, so assume that
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}.
We will show that there is an edge
y\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
such that
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{y}
. For each
v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
, let
w_{i,k}
be the projection
w_{i,k}=\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k}^{x}}v_{i}
. By hypothesis each
w_{i,k}
is either primitive or zero. Assume without loss of generality that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,2,0}\right|
is maximal over all
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}\right|
. By possibly replacing
v_{1}
with
-v_{1}
, we may also assume without loss of generality that
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,2,0}(x)\geq 0
. Since
g(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x})\geq 13
and the set
\{w_{1,0},\ldots,w_{8,0},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\}
has eleven elements, we may choose two primitive classes
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
such that the following hold:

(1)

\langle u_{t},w_{i,0}\rangle=0
for all
1\leq t\leq 2,1\leq i\leq 8
,

(2)

\langle u_{t},r_{s}\rangle=0
for all
1\leq t\leq 2,1\leq s\leq 3
,

(3)

\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle=1
,

Consider the following classes
h_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
:

(1)

h_{1}=w_{1,0}-u_{1}
,

(2)

h_{2}=w_{2,0}-u_{2}
and

(3)

h_{i}=w_{i,0}
for
3\leq i\leq 8
.

Now, let
y
be an edge that shares a 2–cell with
x
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
,

(2)

h_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
,

(3)

r_{s}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}
for
1\leq s\leq 3
, and

(4)

u_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}
for
1\leq t\leq 2
.

We will show that:

(1)

y\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
and

(2)

r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in A_{y}
.

This completes the proof, since then
r_{1}\wedge r_{2}\wedge r_{3}\in\im(\varphi)
. The latter property follows by construction, so it suffices to prove the former. Let
z
denote the third edge of a 2–cell
\sigma
with
x\subseteq\sigma
,
y\subseteq\sigma
. Label
\mathcal{H}(z)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}\}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
. Therefore we have
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}=\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}
. For notational convenience, let
u_{i}=0
for
3\leq i\leq 8
. By construction, the vectors
\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k}^{w}}(v_{i})
for each choice of
1\leq i\leq 8
,
0\leq k\leq 1
and
w=x,y,z
are given as follows:

(1)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{y}}(v_{i})=h_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
,

(2)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{y}}(v_{i})=u_{i}+w_{i,1}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
,

(3)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{0}^{z}}(v_{i})=u_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 8
, and

(4)

\proj_{[b]^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}^{z}}(v_{i})=h_{i}+w_{i,1}
for
1\leq i\leq 8.

Now, given these projections, we can compute ranks and intersection numbers as follows.

(1)

We have
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y),\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(z)\leq 1
for
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
, since by assumption and construction each projection is primitive.

(2)

We have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(y)\right|=\left|\langle h_{i},h_{j}\rangle\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(x)\right|-\mathbbm{1}_{(i,j)=(1,2)}.

(3)

We have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(z)\right|=\left|\langle u_{i},u_{j}\rangle\right|=\mathbbm{1}_{(i,j)=(1,2)}.

Furthermore, since
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=0
by assumption, we have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(y)\right|=\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(y)\right|
, and similarly for
z
. Therefore we have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\right|\geq\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(y)\right|,\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(z)\right|
for all
1\leq i,j\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
. Additionally, we have assumed that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(x)\right|>1
, so we have
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,2,0}(x)\right|>\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,2,0}(y)\right|,\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{1,2,0}(z)\right|
.
Hence we have
y\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
, so the proof is complete.
∎

We now complete the second step of the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Let
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
be a nonseparating multicurve with
\left|\pi_{0}(M)\right|=8
. Let
b\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve such that
b
has geometric intersection number with
a
, and such that
b
intersects
M
trivially. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
. Let
(x,f)\in\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
be a class such that
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 8
and
0\leq k\leq 1
. Then there is a relation in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by

(x,f)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\lambda_{\ell}(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})

such that
\lambda_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(y_{\ell})\leq 1
, and
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(y_{\ell})\leq 1
for all
1\leq\ell\leq m
,
1\leq i,j\leq 8
, and
0\leq k\leq 1
.

Proof.

Let
\maxalg(x)=\max_{1\leq i,j\leq 8}\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(x)\right|
. Since
\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}(x)=-\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,1}(x)
, we only need take the maximum for
k=0
. Let
\nummaxalg(x)
denote the number of pairs
1\leq i<j\leq 8
such that
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,0}\right|=\maxalg(x)
. The proof proceeds by double induction on
\maxalg(x)
and
\nummaxalg(x)
.

Base case:
\maxalg(x)\leq 1
. In this case, the relation in the lemma is the trivial relation
(x,f)=(x,f)
, so the lemma holds.

Inductive step: the lemma holds for all
y\subseteq X_{g}
with either
\maxalg(y)<\maxalg(x)
or with both
\maxalg(y)\leq\maxalg(x)
and
\nummaxalg(y)<\nummaxalg(x)
. By Lemma
7.7
, there is a linear combination

f=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}

such that each
f_{\ell}\in A_{y_{\ell}}
, where
y_{\ell}\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
. By definition, each
y_{\ell}
shares a 2–cell
\sigma_{\ell}
with
x
, and
f_{\ell}\in A_{\sigma_{\ell}}
. Let
z_{\ell}
denote the third edge of
\sigma_{\ell}
. Then there is a relation in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by

\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}d_{2,1}^{1}(\sigma_{\ell},f_{\ell})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(x,f_{\ell})+(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})-(z_{\ell},f_{\ell})=0.

By rearranging terms and applying the fact that
f=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{\ell}
, we have

(x,f)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}(z_{\ell},f_{\ell})-(y_{\ell},f_{\ell}).

But then
y_{\ell}\in\algshrink_{\mathcal{V}}(x)
for all
1\leq\ell\leq m
, so for all
1\leq\ell\leq m
, either
\maxalg(y_{\ell})<\maxalg(x)
or
\maxalg(y_{\ell})=\maxalg(x)
and
\nummaxalg(y_{\ell})<\nummaxalg(x_{\ell})
, and similarly for
z_{\ell}
. Therefore the classes
(y_{\ell},f_{\ell})
and
(z_{\ell},f_{\ell})
are linear combinations of classes as in the statement of the lemma by the inductive hypothesis, so the lemma holds for
(x,f)
as well.
∎

We are now ready to conclude Section
7
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
denote the subspace of
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
spanned by elements
(x,f)
where
x
satisfies
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x),\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<j\leq 8
,
0\leq k\leq 1
. We will show that
W=\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
. By Lemma
7.4
, it suffices to show that
(x,f)\in W
for any
x\subseteq X_{g}
and
f\in A_{x}
.
This follows from Lemmas
7.6
and
7.8
. Now, as a consequence of Lemma
7.2
, there is a finite set of edges
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\subseteq X_{g}
given by all possible combinations of genera
g(\mathcal{H}(y_{\ell}))
and choices of
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}
and
\left|\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}\right|
less than or equal to one, such that any
x\subseteq X_{g}
with
\rk^{\mathcal{V}}_{i,k}(x),\theta(\mathcal{V})_{i,j,k}(x)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i<j\leq 8
,
0\leq k\leq 1
is in the same
G
–orbit as some
y_{\ell}
, so the natural map

\bigoplus_{1\leq\ell\leq n}\Ind_{\Stab_{G}(y_{\ell})}^{G}A_{y_{\ell}}\rightarrow W

is surjective. Therefore the map

H_{0}\left(G;\bigoplus_{1\leq\ell\leq n}\Ind_{\Stab_{G}(y_{\ell})}^{G}A_{y_{\ell}}\right)\rightarrow H_{0}(G;W)

is surjective since
H_{0}(G,-)
is left exact. Then Shapiro’s lemma says that

H_{0}\left(G;\bigoplus_{1\leq\ell\leq n}\Ind_{\Stab_{G}(y_{\ell})}^{G}A_{y_{i}}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{1\leq\ell\leq n}H_{0}(\Stab_{G}(y_{\ell});A_{y_{\ell}}).

But then
A_{y_{\ell}}
is contained in
H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})
for all
y_{\ell}
, so
A_{y_{\ell}}
is finite dimensional for all
y_{\ell}
. Therefore
H_{0}(\Stab_{G}(y_{\ell});A_{y_{\ell}})
is finite dimensional for any
1\leq\ell\leq n
, so the proof is complete.
∎

8.
The Proof of Theorem
B

In this section, we will complete the proof of Proposition
1.2
, which, along with Proposition
1.1
, completes the proof of Theorem
B
. For the remainder of this section, unless otherwise specified, fix a
g\geq 51
and
a\subseteq S_{g}
a nonseparating curve. We will also let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
C_{[a]}(S_{g})
.

The outline of Section
8
. We will devote the bulk of the section to proving Lemma
8.1
, which is done in Section
8.1
. The statement of Lemma
8.1
requires some notation, so we defer the statement for a moment. We then use Lemma
7.1
and
8.1
to prove Proposition
1.2
in Section
8.2
, and then use Proposition
1.2
and Proposition
1.1
to prove Theorem
B
.

8.1.
The proof of Lemma
8.1

Let
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
be a set of primitive elements. Let
\tau_{g}
denote the Johnson homomorphism. Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge, and let
A_{x}^{\mathcal{V}}
denote the subspace of
\im(\tau_{g})=\wedge^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
given by

\left(\im\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\oplus\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\rightarrow\im(\tau_{g})\right)\cap\im\left(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}\mathcal{V}^{\perp}\rightarrow\im(\tau_{g})\right)\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}.

Let
X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq X_{g}
denote the subcomplex consisting of cells
\sigma
such that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is
compatible with
\mathcal{V}
, i.e., every
v\in\mathcal{V}
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{H}
for some
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,\mathcal{V}}
denote the image of the composition

\bigoplus_{x\in\left(X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}}\right)^{(1)}}A_{x}^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}

where
A_{x}
is an in Section
7
. If
\mathcal{V}=\{v\}
is a singleton, we will denote
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,\mathcal{V}}
and
A_{x}^{\mathcal{V}}
by
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,v}
and
A_{x}^{v}
respectively. We are now ready to state Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{9}\}\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
be a set of primitive elements such that there is a nonseparating multicurve
M\subseteq S_{g}{\ssearrow}a
with
\mathcal{V}=\{[c]:c\in\pi_{0}(M)\}
. Then the natural map

\xi:\bigoplus_{v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,v_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}

is surjective.

The outline of the proof of Lemma
8.1
. We will prove Lemmas
8.2
,
8.3
and
8.6
. The first two lemmas are statements about generating sets for vector spaces equipped with alternating forms, while the last is a statement about a generating set for the first rational homology of a certain subcomplex of
X_{g}
. In order to prove Lemma
8.6
, we will prove Lemma
8.4
and Lemma
8.5
, which are auxiliary results about rational abelianizations of subgroups and quotients of the Torelli group. We use Lemma
8.2
, Lemma
8.3
, and Lemma
8.6
to prove Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
–vector space equipped with an alternating form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Let
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
be elements in
V
such that the image of the set
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
under the adjoint map
V\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Q}}(V,\mathbb{Q})
is linearly independent. Then the natural map

\psi:\bigoplus_{i\in\{1,2,3\}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}v_{i}^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V

is surjective.

Proof.

This follows by a dimension count. Let
\mathcal{B}=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
be a basis for
V
such that
\langle v_{i},a_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\{a_{1},\ldots,\widehat{a_{i}},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, which is a basis for
v_{i}^{\perp}
for any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. The vector space
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V
has a basis consisting of pairs of elements in
\mathcal{B}
. Each pair of these elements is contained in at least one
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Therefore
\im(\psi)
contains a basis for
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V
, so
\psi
is surjective.
∎

We now extend Lemma
8.2
as follows.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
–vector space equipped with an alternating form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Let
\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
be a set of elements in
V
with
\left|\mathcal{V}\right|\geq 3
such that the image of
\mathcal{V}
under the adjoint map
V\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}(V,\mathbb{Z})
is linearly independent. Let
m\leq\left|\mathcal{V}\right|-2
be a natural number. Then the natural map

\psi_{m}:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}:\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=m}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\left(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right)^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V

is surjective.

Proof.

This follows by induction on
m
.

Base case:
m=1
. This follows from Lemma
8.2
.

Inductive step: the lemma holds for
m^{\prime}<m
. Let
\psi_{m}
be as in the lemma. By the inductive hypothesis, the map
\psi_{m-1}
is surjective. Hence it suffices to show that
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\right)\subseteq\im(\psi_{m})
for any
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\left|\mathcal{W}\right|=m-1
. Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be a subset with
\left|\mathcal{W}\right|=m-1
. Since we have chosen
m
with
m\leq\left|\mathcal{V}\right|-2
, we have
\left|\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}\right|\geq 3
. Hence Lemma
8.2
applied to
L=\mathcal{W}^{\perp}
and the set
\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
says that the map
