\bigoplus_{v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(v^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\perp})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}

is surjective. Since
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(v^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\perp})\subseteq\im(\varphi_{m})
for any
v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{W}
because
\left|\{v\}\cup\mathcal{W}\right|=m
, we have
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\subseteq\im(\psi_{m})
. Hence
\im(\psi_{m})=\im(\psi_{m-1})
and the inductive hypothesis says that
\im(\psi_{m-1})
is surjective, so
\im(\psi_{m})
is surjective as well.
∎

We will now give an explicit description of the vector space
H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. We first give an alternate description of the Johnson homomorphism.

The Johnson homomorphism, alternate version
. Let
\pi_{1}^{(k)}(S_{g})
denote the
k
th term of the lower central series of the fundamental group of
\pi_{1}(S_{g})
(we suppress the basepoint in the notation, since the choice of basepoint does not affect the construction). The
Johnson homomorphism
is a map

\tau_{g}:\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(\pi_{1}(S_{g})/\pi_{1}^{(1)}(S_{g}),\pi_{1}^{(1)}(S_{g})/\pi_{1}^{(2)}(S_{g})\right).

If
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S_{g})
is a loop and
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
is a mapping class, then
\tau_{g}(f)(\gamma)=\gamma^{-1}f(\gamma)
, where the element
\gamma^{-1}f(\gamma)
is only defined up to conjugation by
\pi_{1}(S_{g})
. Johnson showed that this is well defined as a map
\pi_{1}(S_{g})/\pi_{1}^{(1)}(S_{g})\rightarrow\pi_{1}^{(1)}(S_{g})/\pi_{1}^{(2)}(S_{g})
. Now, if
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
is given by
a_{1}\wedge b_{1}+\ldots+a_{g}\wedge b_{g}
for
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq g}
a symplectic basis for
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
, then the Johnson homomorphism can be rewritten as a map

\tau_{g}:\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z}),\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}\omega^{\prime}\right).

This description of the Johnson homomorphism allows us to prove Lemma
8.4
. If
\mathcal{W}\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
is a set of elements, recall that
X_{g}^{\mathcal{W}}\subseteq X_{g}
denotes the subcomplex of
X_{g}
generated by elements
\sigma
such that
\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
is compatible with
\mathcal{W}
, i.e., for each
w\in\mathcal{W}
there there is an
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
such that
w\in\mathcal{H}
. If
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}
are a symplectic basis for
H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})
with
[a]=\alpha_{1}
, let
\omega^{\prime}_{a}=\alpha_{2}\wedge\beta_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{g}\wedge\beta_{g}
. If
V\subseteq H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is a subspace, let
X_{g}^{V}
denote the subcomplex of
X_{g}
consisting of cells
x
such that
V\subseteq\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
for some
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(\sigma)
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
g\geq 4
and let
a\subseteq S_{g}
be a nonseparating simple closed curve. Then there is an isomorphism
\overline{\tau_{g}}:H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}\omega^{\prime}_{a}
. Furthermore, this isomorphism is functorial in the following sense. Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
be a set of elements such that
g(\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\geq 1
. Then
\im(H_{1}(X_{g}^{\mathcal{W}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}\omega_{a}^{\prime})\supseteq\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}\omega_{a}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
g\geq 4
, the complex
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
is
1
–acyclic by a theorem of the author
[
21
, Theorem A]
. Therefore the last three terms of the five term exact sequence associated to the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
form a right exact sequence

H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow 0.

We have the following claim.

Claim
. We have
\im(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))=\ker(\eval_{[a]}\circ\tau_{g})
, where

\eval_{[a]}:\ho_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z}),\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}\omega^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}\omega^{\prime}

is the linear map given by evaluation on
[a]
.

Proof of claim
. We prove each containment in turn. If
f\in\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a)
, we have
\tau_{g}(f)([a])=0
, so
f\in\ker(\eval_{[a]})
and the
\subseteq
containment holds. For the
\supseteq
containment, let
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
be a mapping class such that
\eval_{[a]}(\tau_{g}(f))=0
. Let
\gamma
be a loop in
\pi_{1}(S_{g})
such that
\gamma
is homotopic to
a
as an unbased loop. Since the choice of representative of
[a]
is arbitrary, we must have
\gamma^{-1}f(\gamma)\in\pi_{1}^{(2)}(S_{g})
since
\tau_{g}(f)(a)=0
by hypothesis. A theorem of Church
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
tells us that there is some
h\in\mathcal{K}_{g}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{g}
is the Johnson kernel
[
8
, Section 6.6]
, such that
ha=f(a)
, so
h^{-1}f(a)=a
, and therefore
h^{-1}f\in\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a)
. Johnson
[
11
, Lemma 4A]
showed that
h\in\ker(\tau_{g})
, so
[h^{-1}f]=[f]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
, since
\im(\tau_{g})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})

[
13
]
. Therefore
[f]\in\im(H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
, so the claim holds.

Given the claim, we have an exact sequence

H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\eval_{[a]}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})/\mathbb{Q}\omega^{\prime}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
\im\left(\eval_{[a]}\right)=\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Q}\omega^{\prime}_{a}
. This is a consequence of Johnson’s computation of the image of the Johnson homomorphism
[
11
, Theorem 1]
.

We now use Lemma
4.4
to prove the second part of the lemma. In particular, it suffices to show that any element
\gamma\wedge\delta\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}
with
\gamma,\delta
primitive and
\langle\gamma,\delta\rangle=1
lies in the image of the composition

H_{1}(X_{g}^{\mathcal{W}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/Q\omega^{\prime}_{a}.

Let
x\subseteq X_{g}
be an edge,
\widehat{x}
a lift of
x
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
with one vertex equal to
a
, and
f\in\mathcal{I}_{g}
a mapping class taking
a
to the other endpoint of
\widehat{x}
. By the construction of the equivariant homology spectral sequence, the class
[x]\in H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the image under the map
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
of the class
[f]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. Then by alternate definition of
\tau_{g}
given before the lemma, we have
\eval_{[a]}(f)=\omega_{\mathcal{H}}
, where
\omega_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is the characteristic element for some
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
. Then for any
\gamma\wedge\delta
with
\gamma,\delta
primitive and
\langle\gamma,\delta\rangle=1
, there is an
x\subseteq X_{g}
and
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
with
\omega_{\mathcal{H}}=\gamma\wedge\delta
. The set of such
\gamma\wedge\delta
spans
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
by Lemma
4.4
, so the image of the composition

H_{1}(X_{g}^{\mathcal{W}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/Q\omega^{\prime}_{a}.

contains
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(\mathcal{W}^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}\omega_{a}^{\prime})
, so the lemma is complete.
∎

We will need another auxiliary lemma about the rational abelianizations of the Torelli groups of surfaces.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
g\geq 4
, and let
\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1}
be two surfaces each equipped with an embedding
\iota^{i}:\mathcal{T}_{i}\hookrightarrow S_{g}
. Assume that the following hold:

•

each embedding
\iota^{i}
is clean,

•

S_{g}{\ssearrow}\iota^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i})
is connected,

•

g(\mathcal{T}_{i})\geq 3
for
i=0,1
,

•

the pullback
S
of the maps
\iota^{0}
and
\iota^{1}
is a connected, smooth manifold,

•

S_{g}{\ssearrow}\left(\iota^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{0})\cap\iota^{1}(\mathcal{T}_{1})\right)
is connected, and

•

g(S)\geq 3
.

The following commuting square

\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S,S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}_{1},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}^{1}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{T}_{2},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}^{2}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}),}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

For each
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, let
\kappa^{i}
denote the map
S\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{i}
, and let
\iota
denote the composition
\iota^{i}\circ\kappa^{i}
. Note that since
S
is a pullback, we have
\iota^{1}\circ\kappa^{1}=\iota^{0}\circ\kappa^{0}
, so the definition of
\iota
does not depend on
i
. Now, since we have assumed that each embedding
\iota^{i}
is clean, we have
\iota^{i}_{*}
injective by a theorem of Putman
[
25
, Theorem B]
. The map
\iota
is also clean by hypothesis, and therefore the pushforward
\iota_{*}
is injective by the same theorem of Putman, so it suffices to show that
\im(\iota_{*})=\im(\iota_{*}^{0})\cap\im(\iota_{*}^{1})
. Lemma
4.5
says that that
\im(\mathcal{I}(\iota^{i})_{*})=\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{i};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
and
\im(\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*})=\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
. Since the functor
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}
from
\mathbb{Q}
–vector spaces to
\mathbb{Q}
–vector spaces sends pullbacks of monomorphisms to pullbacks of monomorphisms, it is enough to show that

\im(H_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{0};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\cap\im(H_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{1};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q}))=\im(H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Q})).

This follows from our hypotheses that
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\iota(S)
and
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\iota^{i}(\mathcal{T}_{i})
are connected, so embedding
S
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}
into
S_{g}
does not introduce any new relations in
H_{1}(S;\mathbb{Q})
or
H_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{i};\mathbb{Q})
.
∎

We now prove the following.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
L\subseteq[a]^{\perp}
be a free abelian subgroup with
g(L)=2
and
\dim(L\otimes\mathbb{Q})=4
. Let
V=L\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\mathcal{V}
be as in Lemma
8.1
. Then the natural map

\psi:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}:|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}|=4}H_{1}(X_{g}^{V}\cap X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})

is surjective.

Proof.

We begin with the following claim.

Claim
. The pushforward
H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is an injection, and the image of this map is sent to
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}
under the isomorphism
H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Q}\omega_{\alpha}
from Lemma
8.4
.

Proof of claim
. Let
S_{2}^{1}\subseteq S_{g}
be a compact subsurface such that
\im\left(H_{1}(S_{2}^{1};\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow H_{1}(S_{g};\mathbb{Z})\right)=V
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1}
denote the subgroup of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
generated by elements that fix
\partial S_{2}^{1}
and restrict to the identity on
S_{2}^{1}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g},S_{2}^{1})
denote the subcomplex of
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
generated by curves
c
such that
c
is disjoint from
S_{2}^{1}
. By a result of Kent, Leininger and Schleimer
[
15
, Theorem 7.2]
, the fibers of the natural map
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g},S_{2}^{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g-2})
are all trees, so
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g},S_{2}^{1})
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g-2})
. Then a theorem of the author
[
21
, Theorem A]
says that
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g-2})
is at least 1–acyclic, and thus
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g},S_{2}^{1})
is at least 1–acyclic as well. Therefore the equivariant homology spectral sequence
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}(\mathcal{I}^{1}_{g-2},\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g},S_{2}^{1});\mathbb{Q})
converges to
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}^{1}_{g-2};\mathbb{Q})
. Hence there is a right exact sequence

H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow 0.

Since
g\geq 51
, we have
g\left(S_{g-2}^{1}{\ssearrow}a\right)\geq 3
. Then the inclusion
S_{g-2}^{1}{\ssearrow}a\hookrightarrow S_{g-2}^{1}
is a clean embedding (as in Section
3
), so a theorem of Putman
[
25
, Theorem B]
says that the pushforward map
H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1};\mathbb{Q})
is an injection. Hence the above right exact sequence is in fact exact:

0\rightarrow H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow 0.

Furthemore, there is a morphism of short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{a}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g-2}^{1};\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\rho}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(a);\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
\rho_{a}
and
\rho
are the natural pushforward maps. Lemma
8.5
says that left square is a pullback. Furthermore, each map in this square is an injection by the aforementioned theorem of Putman, so the map
\cok(\rho_{a})\rightarrow\cok(\rho)
is injective. Furthermore, since
\rho
is injective, we have
\ker(\rho)=0
. Then the snake lemma says that there is an exact sequence

\ker(\rho)\rightarrow\ker(\overline{\rho})\rightarrow\cok(\rho_{a})\rightarrow\cok(\rho).

The map
\cok(\rho_{a})\rightarrow\cok(\rho)
is injective and
\ker(\rho)=0
, so
\ker(\overline{\rho})=0
, and in particular we that
\overline{\rho}:H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is an injection. The second part of the claim follows from the fact that for any edge
x\subseteq X_{g}^{V}
, the characteristic element
\omega_{\mathcal{H}}
for any
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{H}(x)
lies in
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, because
V
is a vector space. This implies that
\im(H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is contained in
\im(\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}V^{\perp}\cap\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. Then the second part of Lemma
8.4
gives the containment in the other direction, so we have equality.

Given the claim, we now continue with the proof of the lemma. The claim says that
H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}[V]^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. Since
\dim(V)=4
and the elements of
\mathcal{V}
have pairwise trivial algebraic intersection, the set
\proj_{V^{\perp}}(\mathcal{V})
contains at least six linearly independent elements. Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be a subset of maximal size with
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\proj_{V^{\perp}}(\mathcal{B})
linearly independent. Since we have assumed that each element
v\in\mathcal{V}
has trivial algebraic intersection with
[a]
, we have
\mathcal{B}\subseteq[a]^{\perp}\cap V^{\perp}
. Then since
\left|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\right|=6
, Lemma
8.3
applied to the set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and the vector space
V^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp}
with
m=4
says that the natural map

\psi:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is a surjection. Then, for any
v\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
v^{\perp}\cap V^{\perp}=w^{\perp}\cap V^{\perp}
, where
w=\proj_{V}(v)
, so the natural map

\psi:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}:\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap(\mathcal{V}^{\prime})^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is a surjection. We have
H_{1}(X_{g}^{V})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap[a]^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
by the claim. Furthermore, by Lemma
8.4
, we have
\im(H_{1}(X_{g}^{V}\cap X_{g}^{v});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\supseteq\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{2}(V^{\perp}\cap v^{\perp})
for any
v\in\mathcal{B}
. Then by substituting in each of these homology groups in the previous equality, we see that the natural map

\psi:\bigoplus_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}:\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4}H_{1}(X_{g}^{V}\cap X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}};\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})

is surjective, so the lemma is complete.
∎

We are now almost ready to conclude Section
8.1
. We will first prove the following auxiliary result, and then we will prove Lemma
8.1
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
(x,f)\in\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}
be a class. There is a linear combination in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by

(x,f)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i},f_{i})

such that, for each
1\leq i\leq m
, each
x_{i}
has a representative
\widehat{x}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
and
f_{i}
has a representative
F_{i}\in\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x}_{i})
. These representatives have the property that there is a subsurface
\mathcal{T}_{i}\subseteq S_{g}
with
\mathcal{T}_{i}\cong S_{2}^{1}
,
F_{i}
supported on
\mathcal{T}_{i}
, and
\widehat{x}_{i}
disjoint from
\mathcal{T}_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{x}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
be a representative for
x
. Let
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}
be the connected components of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{x}
. We have a surjection
\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})\times\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime\prime},S_{g})\rightarrow\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x})
, and hence by the Künneth formula we have a surjection

H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\oplus H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{1}(\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x});\mathbb{Q}).

Hence we may assume that, without loss of generality,
f
is represented by a mapping class
F\in\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
. Furthermore, we may assume that
g(S^{\prime})\geq 4
. Indeed, if
g(S^{\prime})\leq 3
, then
g\geq 51\geq 7
implies that there is a 2–cell
\widehat{\sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
such that
\widehat{x}\subseteq\widehat{\sigma}
, and such that for
\widehat{y},\widehat{z}\subseteq\widehat{\sigma}
the other two edges, the connected component of
S_{g}{\ssearrow}\widehat{y}
that contains
S^{\prime}
has genus at least 3, and similarly for
\widehat{z}
. Then since
g(S^{\prime})\geq 4\geq 3
, Lemma
4.3
says that
\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
is generated by bounding pair maps. Then bounding pair maps supported on separating curves vanish in
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
since Dehn twists along separating curves vanish under
\tau_{g}
. Then Lemma
4.5
says that if
\iota:S^{\prime}\rightarrow S_{g}
is the inclusion map, the pushforward
\mathcal{I}(\iota)_{*}
in
H_{1}
is injective, so bounding pair maps supported on separating curves are trivial in
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
. Hence the class
f
is a linear combination of classes represented by bounding pair maps supported on nonseparating curves contained in
S^{\prime}
, so we may assume that
f
has a representative
T_{c,c^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
for
c\cup c^{\prime}
a bounding pair with
[c]\neq 0
. We now have two cases.

Case 1:
[c]\neq[a]
. We first show that we can assume that no connected component of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime})
has genus zero. Suppose otherwise, so one connected component of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime})
is a subsurface
P
with
P\cong S_{0}^{4}
. Since we have assumed that
g(S^{\prime})\geq 4\geq 3
, there is another curve
c^{\prime\prime}
disjoint from
c\cup c^{\prime}
and not equal to
c
or
c^{\prime\prime}
with
[c^{\prime\prime}]=[c]
and such that both connected components of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime\prime})
have positive genus. Now, we have
T_{c,c^{\prime}}=T_{c,c^{\prime\prime}}T_{c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime}}
, and both
c\cup c^{\prime\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}\cup c^{\prime}
satisfied the desired condition on the genera of connected components. Now, assuming that both connected components of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime})
have positive genus, we can rewrite
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
as a product of bounding pair maps
T_{c_{0},c_{1}}T_{c_{1},c_{2}}\ldots T_{c_{n-1},c_{n}}
such that:

•

c_{0}=c
,
c_{n}=c^{\prime}
, and

•

at least one connected component of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c_{i}\cup c_{i+1})
has genus one.

Hence we may assume without loss of generality that
c\cup c^{\prime}
is supported on a surface of genus one with two boundary components. Then there is a surface
\mathcal{T}
that contains
c\cup c^{\prime}
and is disjoint from
\widehat{x}
such that
\mathcal{T}\cong S_{2}^{1}
, as desired.

Case 2:
[c]=[a]
. We will reduce to Case 1, by showing that
[T_{c,c^{\prime}}]\in H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
is a sum
[T_{d,d^{\prime}}]+[T_{e,e^{\prime}}]
with
d\cup d^{\prime}
and
e\cup e^{\prime}
bounding pairs such that
[d],[e]\neq[a]
. As in Case 1, we may assume that the connected component of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime})
that does not contain
\widehat{x}
has genus one. Then by Lemma
4.5
, we have
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g});\mathbb{Q})\cong\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
. By Lemma
4.2
, if the connected component
S^{\prime\prime}
of
S^{\prime}{\ssearrow}(c\cup c^{\prime})
that does not contain
\widehat{x}
has
a,b\in H_{1}(S^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{Z})
a pair of primitive elements with
\langle a,b\rangle=1
, then the image of
T_{c,c^{\prime}}
in
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Q})
is
[a]\wedge[b]\wedge[c]
. Now, choose nonzero primitive
[d],[e]\in H_{1}(S^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
such that
[d]+[e]=[c]
and
[d],[e]\in[a]^{\perp}\cap[b]^{\perp}
. Then we take
T_{d,d^{\prime}}
and
T_{e,e^{\prime}}
bounding pair maps in
\mathcal{I}(S^{\prime},S_{g})
such that
[T_{d,d^{\prime}}]=[a]\wedge[b]\wedge[d]
and
[T_{e,e^{\prime}}]=[a]\wedge[b]\wedge[e]
.
∎

We are now ready to conclude Section
8.1
.

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Let
(x,f)\in\bigoplus_{x\in X_{g}^{(1)}}A_{x}
be a class, where
x\subseteq X_{g}
is an edge and
f\in A_{x}
. Let
\widehat{x}
be a lift of
x
to
\mathcal{C}_{[a]}(S_{g})
such that
a
is a vertex of
\widehat{x}
. We will show that the image of
(x,f)
in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is contained in
\im(\xi)
. By Lemma
8.7
, it suffices to prove the result in the case that
f
is represented by a bounding pair map
F\in\Stab_{\mathcal{I}_{g}}(\widehat{x})
and that there is an inclusion
\iota:S_{2}^{1}\hookrightarrow S_{g}
such that
\widehat{x}
is disjoint from
\im(\iota)
and
F
is supported on
\im(\iota)
. Let
V=\im(\iota_{*})
. By Lemma
8.6
, there is a linear combination in
H_{1}(X_{g}^{V};\mathbb{Q})
given by

[x]=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}[x_{i}]

such that each
x_{i}
is contained in
X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\cap X_{g}^{V}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
. Since each
x_{i}\subseteq X_{g}^{V}
, we have
f\in A_{x_{i}}
for each
x_{i}
, and thus there is a linear combination in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
given by

(x,f)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(x_{i},f).

Hence it is enough to prove the result in the case that
x\in X_{g}^{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
for some
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\left|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\right|=4
. Let
\mathcal{H}(x)=\{\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{x}\}
and, without loss of generality, assume
V\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
. Now, if
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\not\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}
for
i=\{0,1\}
, we have
(x,f)\in\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,v}
for any
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the vector space
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and the set
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
4.8
. Hence there is a linear combination

f=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}f_{j}

where each
f_{j}\in\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}^{3}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{x}\cap v_{i}^{\perp})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
for some
v_{i}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Hence in
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
, we have

(x,f)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\lambda_{j}(x,f_{j})

with each
(x,f_{j})\in\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2,v}
for some
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
. Therefore we have
(x,f)\in\im(\xi)
as desired.
∎

8.2.
The proof of Theorem
B

We begin by showing that
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is finite dimensional.

Proof of Proposition
1.2
.

Let
G=\im(\Mod(S_{g}{\ssearrow}a)\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z}))
. We will show that the hypotheses of Proposition
2.1
are satisfied for the
G
–representation
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
with
d=9
. The first hypothesis is exactly Lemma
8.1
and the second is exactly Lemma
7.1
, so
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is finite dimensional by Proposition
2.1
.
∎

We are now ready to complete Section
8
.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Let
g\geq 51
and
\mathbb{E}_{*,*}^{*}
denote the equivariant homology spectral sequence for the action of
\mathcal{I}_{g}
on
C_{[a]}(S_{g})
. By a result of the author
[
21
, Theorem A]
,
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
converges to
H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
for
p+q=2
. Hence it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
are finite dimensional. The vector space
\mathbb{E}_{2,0}^{2}
is finite dimensional by Proposition
1.1
and
\mathbb{E}_{1,1}^{2}
is finite dimensional by Proposition
1.2
.
∎

9.
The proof of Theorem
A

We now prove Theorem
A
. We will begin by proving Lemma
9.1
, which is an alternate proof of a corollary of a result of Kassabov and Putman result
[
14
, Theorem A]
. We will then use Theorem
B
along with Proposition
2.1
to prove Theorem
A
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
g\geq 3
. The vector space
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{r}
be the Leray–Serre spectral sequence associated to the short exact sequence

1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{g}\rightarrow\Mod(S_{g})\rightarrow\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1.

The vector space
H_{2}(\Mod(S_{g});\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional
[
8
, Section 5.4]
. Then since
H_{1}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
is finite dimensional
[
12
]
, the modules
\mathbb{E}_{p,q}^{2}=H_{p}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{q}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
are all finite dimensional for
q\leq 1

[
27
, Corollary 3]
. Hence both the image and the kernel of the pushforward map

H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))\rightarrow H_{2}(\Mod(S_{g});\mathbb{Q})

are finite dimensional, so
H_{0}(\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z});H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q}))
is finite dimensional.
∎

We now prove the main result of the paper.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

Let
G=\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})
and
V=H_{2}(\mathcal{I}_{g};\mathbb{Q})
. We will show that the
G
–representation
V
satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
2.1
for
d=1
, and hence is finite dimensional. The first hypothesis is the content of Theorem
B
, while the second is the content of Lemma
9.1
.
∎
